{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{S}(\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}):=\underset{\delta\bm{u}_{k}\in\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{u}}}{\operatorname{min}}\ \nabla^{S}(\delta\bm{u}_{k}).

(2.53)

Since
\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}
is a minimum of the plant,

\text{ \eqref{eq:2___29_NablaS_Signe} }\Rightarrow\quad 1-\nabla^{S}(\bm{u}_{p}^{\star},\delta\bm{u}_{k})>0,\ \forall\delta\bm{u}_{k}\in\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{u}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad 1-\hbox to9.73pt{\vbox to10.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.86667pt\lower-5.61665pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.16667pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -6.04 -7.08 L 6.04 -7.08}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{S}(\bm{u}_{p}^{\star})>0.

Therefore, the stability condition is a condition on
K
:

0<K<\frac{2}{1-\hbox to9.73pt{\vbox to10.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.86667pt\lower-5.61665pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.16667pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -6.04 -7.08 L 6.04 -7.08}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{S}(\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}))},

(2.54)

and this set is never empty since
1-\nabla^{S}(\bm{u}_{p}^{\star},\delta\bm{u}_{k})>0
.

Finally, to choose a “small enough” filter one just needs to choose
K
such that the inequality (
2.54
) is satisfied. However, it is clear that it is a priori impossible to evaluate this inequality because to do so one would need to know both
\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}
and the directional derivatives
\nabla^{S}
of the function
\bm{sol}
, and neither of them are a priori known.

2.7.3
Towards an adaptive filter

To get around this problem, one proposes (i) to compute at each iteration an approximation
\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}_{k}^{S}
of
\hbox to9.73pt{\vbox to10.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.86667pt\lower-5.61665pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.16667pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -6.04 -7.08 L 6.04 -7.08}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{S}(\bm{u}_{p}^{\star})
and (ii) to choose at each iteration a filter
K_{k}
such that:

\displaystyle K_{k}<\

\displaystyle\frac{2}{1-\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k}},

\displaystyle\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}_{k}^{S}:=\

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\bm{u}_{k}-\bm{u}_{k-1}}{\|\bm{u}_{k}-\bm{u}_{k-1}\|}\right)^{\rm T}\frac{\bm{u}^{*}_{k+1}-\bm{u}^{*}_{k}}{\|\bm{u}_{k}-\bm{u}_{k-1}\|},

\displaystyle\text{si }\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}_{k}^{S}<1.

(2.55)

Any strategy of adapting the filter
K
that follows (
2.55
) provides interesting properties in particular:

Theorem 2.4
.

If the conditions of applicability of the Theorem
2.3
are satisfied and if:

•

The problem is unidimensional, i.e.
n_{u}=1
;

•

The function
sol
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
at
\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}
;

•

A filter
K>0
is applied by following the procedure (
2.51
).

Then any filter adaptation strategy that satisfies (
2.55
) guarantees that the stability condition (
2.54
) is always satisfied.

Proof.

Given that
u_{p}^{\star}
is a minimum of the plant, that the function
sol
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
at
u_{p}^{\star}
, and that Theorem
2.3
is applicable, the function
sol
can be approximated by:

\displaystyle sol(u)=\

\displaystyle u_{p}^{\star}+\nabla_{u}sol|_{u_{p}^{\star}}(u-u_{p}^{\star}),

\displaystyle\forall\bm{u}\in\amsmathbb{D}_{r}=[u_{p}^{\star}-r,\ u_{p}^{\star}+r].

(2.56)

In domain
\amsmathbb{D}_{r}
, there is always a sub-domain
\amsmathbb{D}_{\epsilon}=[u_{p}^{\star}-\epsilon,\ u_{p}^{\star}+\epsilon]
such that whatever is the filter
K>0
used,
u_{k}\in\amsmathbb{D}_{\epsilon}\ \Rightarrow\ u_{k+1}\in\amsmathbb{D}_{r}
. Indeed,

\begin{array}[]{rrcl}&u_{p}^{\star}-r<&u_{k+1}&<u_{p}^{\star}+r\\
\Leftrightarrow&u_{p}^{\star}-r<&u_{k}+K(sol(u_{k})-u_{k})&<u_{p}^{\star}+r\\
\Leftrightarrow&u_{p}^{\star}-r<&u_{k}+K(u_{p}^{\star}+\nabla_{u}sol|_{u_{p}^{\star}}(u_{k}-u_{p}^{\star})-u_{k})&<u_{p}^{\star}+r\\
\Leftrightarrow&u_{p}^{\star}-\epsilon<&u_{k}&<u_{p}^{\star}+\epsilon\\
\end{array}

where

\epsilon:=\frac{r}{|1-K+K\nabla_{u}sol|_{u_{p}^{\star}}|}.

So, if an iterate
u_{k}\in\amsmathbb{D}_{\epsilon}
, then
\forall K>0
the next iterate will satisfy:
u_{k+1}\in\amsmathbb{D}_{\delta}
. Given these two iterations and because (i) locally (
2.56
) holds, (ii)
sol
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, (iii)
n_{u}=1
, the estimate
\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}_{k}^{S}
is equal to the actual value of
\nabla_{u}sol|_{u_{p}^{\star}}
.
Therefore, it is sure that the filter chosen for the iteration
u_{k+2}
satisfies (
2.54
). As a result,
u_{k+2}\in\amsmathbb{D}_{r}
and for all the following iterations (
2.54
) will be systematically respected. It follows from this observation that the sequence
\{u_{k+1},...,u_{\infty}\}
converges asymptotically on
u_{p}^{\star}
.

Finally, it is enough that one iterate falls in the domain
\amsmathbb{R}_{\epsilon}
so that the filter chosen with (
2.55
) guarantees that the next iterates converge asymptotically on
\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}
.
Noting that
\amsmathbb{D}_{r}
is a ball
\mathcal{B}(\bm{u}_{p}^{\star},r\rightarrow 0)
in
\amsmathbb{R}
, it can be concluded that any strategy that satisfies (
2.55
) guarantees the satisfaction of the stability condition (
2.54
).
∎

Hereafter, a graphical interpretation of Theorem
2.4
is proposed:

Graphical Interpretation 2.1
.

If the conditions of applicability of Theorem
2.4
are satisfied by a RTO method and if no filter is used.
Then,
\forall u_{o}
close to a stationary point of the plant
u_{p}^{\bullet}
the iterations
u_{k>0}
will follow a behavior similar to one of the 4 cases shown in Figure
2.4
. The four cases of Figure
2.4
correspond to situations where:

case A:

\displaystyle\ \nabla_{u}sol|_{u_{p}^{\star}}\in]1,+\infty[,

case B:

\displaystyle\ \nabla_{u}sol|_{u_{p}^{\star}}\in]0,1[,

case C:

\displaystyle\ \nabla_{u}sol|_{u_{p}^{\star}}\in]-1,0[,

case D:

\displaystyle\ \nabla_{u}sol|_{u_{p}^{\star}}\in]-\infty,-1].

Figure 2.4:
Four possible cases when
n_{u}=1
and
sol
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
at
u_{p}^{\bullet}
.

The black line represents the function
sol(u)
. This line crosses the point
[u_{p}^{\bullet},u_{p}^{\bullet}]
because according to Theorem
2.3

sol(u_{p}^{\bullet})=u_{p}^{\bullet}
. The evolution of the iterations is represented by yellow points on this curve.
(One starts from
\delta u_{k}=\delta u_{0}
to generate the first point
[\delta u_{0},sol(u_{p}^{\bullet}+\delta u_{0})]^{\rm T}
. As no filter is applied, the ordinate of one point is the abscissa of the next one. So, to construct a new point (i.e. move forward one iteration) one must horizontally project a point on the line
y=\delta u_{k}+u_{p}^{\bullet}
(black dotted line) to then project it vertically on the curve
u_{k+1}=sol(u_{p}^{\bullet}+\delta u_{k})
.)
Finally, a color code is used to clearly show in which case (A,B,C,D) one is located. If the function
sol
is in the green domain, then it is the B or C and a filter is not necessary to make the RTO method stable, i.e.
\nabla_{u}sol|_{u_{p}^{\bullet}}\in[-1,1]
which can be linked to (
2.49
). On the other hand, if it is in the yellow or red areas, then one can observe that the iterations do not converge on
u_{p}^{\bullet}
.

Now, let’s build scenarios A’ and D’ similar to scenarios A and D but using a filter
(i.e. “where each horizontal projection is reduced”)
. Figure
2.5
illustrates what is obtained and two things can be observed:

(Case D’:) If the function
sol
is in the yellow area (i.e. if
\nabla_{u}sol|_{u_{p}^{\bullet}}<-1
) then a filter can provide stability. The figure on the very right of Figure
2.5
gives a graphical interpretation of the appropriate choices of
K
.

(Case A’:) If the function
sol
is in the red area (i.e. if
\nabla_{u}sol|_{u_{p}^{\bullet}}>1
), then there is no
K>0
providing stability. However, according to the Theorem
2.3
,
\nabla_{u}sol|_{u_{p}^{\bullet}}>1
implies that
u_{p}^{\bullet}
s not a minimum of the plant.
So this particular case, is a case for which the convergence on
u_{p}^{\bullet}
is not desirable. Hence the interest in setting a lower bound on the choice of
K
to
0
.

Figure 2.5:
Illustration of the effects of the filter.

Finally, the green, yellow and red cones whose common vertex is
u_{p}^{\bullet}
provide the following information: “If for a point
u
the point
[u,sol(u)]
is within the cone of color

•

green, then
sol(u)
will be
closer
to
u_{p}^{\bullet}
than
u
.

•

yellow, then
sol(u)
will be
closer
to
u_{p}^{\bullet}
than
u

if a small enough filter is used
.

•

red, then
sol(u)
will be
farther
from
u_{p}^{\bullet}
than
u
.

These color cones are reused later in this chapter.

2.7.4
Towards an optimal adaptive filter

To go further, one can take the equation (
2.52
) and choose the filter
K
which minimizes the term “
1-K\big(1-\nabla^{S}(\bm{u}_{p}^{\star},\delta\bm{u}_{k})\big)
” so that
\bm{u}_{k+1}
is as close as possible to
\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}
. Of course,
\nabla^{S}(\bm{u}_{p}^{\star},\delta\bm{u}_{k})
is unknown but can be approximated with (
2.55
). So, the idea is to define the filter to be used as the solution of the following optimization problem:

K^{\star}_{k}=\operatorname{arg}\ \underset{K}{\operatorname{min}}\quad\left|1-K\left[1-\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k}\right]\right|,\quad\text{s.t.}\quad K>K_{o},\quad\text{si }\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}_{k}^{S}<1.

(2.57)

where
K_{o}>0
is the minimum value that the filter can take, which must be chosen by the engineers supervising the plant. The default choice is set to
K_{o}:=0.1
. This optimization problem has a simple explicit solution which is:

\displaystyle K^{\star}_{k}=\

\displaystyle\max\left\{K_{o},\frac{1}{1-\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k}}\right\},

\displaystyle\text{if }\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}_{k}^{S}<1.

(2.58)

Notice that by definition
K_{k}^{\star}
always satisfies the condition (
2.55
) because it is obvious that it is always true that:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k}}<\

\displaystyle\frac{2}{1-\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k}},

\displaystyle\text{if: }\qquad\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k}<1.

.

Let’s illustrate the effects of using this adaptive filter on two purely mathematical examples.

•

Example
2.1
is a detailed analysis of the solving of a 1D problem (
n_{u}=1
) satisfying all the conditions of Theorem
2.4
. Some practical points related to the implementation, such as convergence management, are discussed.

•

Example
2.2
proposes an empirical analyses of the use of the adaptive filter (
2.58
). It is observed that very interesting behaviors emerges from the use of this adaptive filter.

Example 2.1
.

(This is an example used in
[
42
]
(the example 4.3 page 112).)
One considers a problem where the functions of the plant and the model are:

\displaystyle\phi(u,y):=\

\displaystyle y,\

\displaystyle y_{p}=f_{p}(u):=\

\displaystyle(u-1)^{2},\

\displaystyle y=f(u):=\

\displaystyle\frac{u^{2}}{4},

and where the constraints are simply on the inputs:
-5\leq u\leq 5
. In this case, it is clear that ISO-D/I are identical and that the equilibrium condition is always satisfied since the cost function is convex at all points and the constraints are known. It is therefore a “simple” case study allowing for the effects of the filter to be analyzed. The rest of this example is divided into two parts. In part 1, a theoretical analysis of the convergence properties of ISO-D/I w.r.t. the filter is proposed. In part 2, simulation results are shown and some "practical" aspects of the implementation of the adaptive filter are discussed.

Part 1: Theoretical analysis

The minimum of the plant is
u_{p}^{\star}=1
and no constraints are active at this point. So the function
sol
given in Figure
2.6
can be approximated around
u_{p}^{\star}
in the following way:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}(u,u_{p}^{\star}):=\

\displaystyle\phi_{p}|_{u_{p}^{\star}}+\nabla_{u}\phi_{p}|_{u_{p}^{\star}}(u-u_{p}^{\star})+\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}_{uu}\phi|_{u_{p}^{\star}}(u-u_{p}^{\star})^{2},

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\nabla_{u}\widetilde{\phi}(u,u_{p}^{\star})=\

\displaystyle\nabla_{u}\phi_{p}|_{u_{p}^{\star}}+\nabla^{2}_{uu}\phi|_{u_{p}^{\star}}(u-u_{p}^{\star}),

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle 0=\

\displaystyle\nabla_{u}\phi_{p}|_{u_{p}^{\star}}+\nabla^{2}_{uu}\phi|_{u_{p}^{\star}}(sol(u_{p}^{\star})-u_{p}^{\star}),

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle sol(u_{p}^{\star})=\

\displaystyle u_{p}^{\star}-\frac{\nabla_{u}\phi_{p}|_{u_{p}^{\star}}}{\nabla^{2}_{uu}\phi|_{u_{p}^{\star}}}.

One can observe on Figure
2.6
(i) that the function
sol(u)
is piecewise-
\mathcal{C}^{1}
and globally
\mathcal{C}^{0}
; (ii) that the function
sol(u)
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
at
u_{p}^{\star}
; and (iii) that a filter will be necessary to bring stability since the curve is in the yellow area around
u_{p}^{\star}
.

\displaystyle\nabla^{S}(u_{p}^{\star},1)=\nabla^{S}(u_{p}^{\star},-1)=\

\displaystyle\nabla_{u_{k}}sol|_{u_{p}^{\star}},

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\nabla^{2}_{uu}\phi_{p}|_{u_{p}^{\star}}\nabla^{2}_{uu}\phi|_{u_{p}^{\star}}-\nabla^{3}_{uuu}\phi|_{u_{p}^{\star}}\nabla_{u}\phi_{p}|_{u_{p}^{\star}}}{\nabla^{2}_{uu}\phi|_{u_{p}^{\star}}^{2}},

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\nabla^{2}_{uu}\phi_{p}|_{u_{p}^{\star}}}{\nabla^{2}_{uu}\phi|_{u_{p}^{\star}}}=1-\frac{2}{1/2}=-3.

Therefore, in order to satisfy the stability condition one should choose the filter in the interval:
K\in]0,\ \frac{2}{1-(-3)}=\frac{1}{2}[
. And according to (
2.58
), the optimal filter is:

K^{\star}=\frac{1}{1-(-3)}=\frac{1}{4}.

(2.59)

Figure 2.6:
Example
2.1
: The function
sol(u)
.

Part 2: Simulations & Implementation.

One runs several simulations starting from the minimum of the model:
u_{0}=0
, and one considers the three following approaches:

The classical approach (MA and MAy)
which consists in letting the engineers supervising the plant predefine the filter to be used. Figure
2.7
shows the results obtained for different filter choices:
K=\{0.1,0.2,0.25,0.3,0.4,

0.5\}
. It can be noticed that
K=0.25
is indeed the best filter since it allows an immediate convergence. On the other hand, all filter
K>0.5
do not enable convergence. So, depending on the choice that is made this approach may, or may not, converge to the plant optimum.

Figure 2.7:
The classical approach: Results for multiple filter choices .

The maximal approach
which consists in choosing, at each iteration, the largest filter which guarantees stability, i.e. at each iteration the filter that is applied is:

\displaystyle\text{If }k=0:

\displaystyle K_{k}=\

\displaystyle K_{o},

(2.60)

\displaystyle\text{If }\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k}<1\text{ et }k>0:

\displaystyle K_{k}=\

\displaystyle\max\Big\{K_{o}:=0.1,\ 2/(1-\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k})-0.1\Big\},

(2.61)

Otherwise:

\displaystyle K_{k}=\

\displaystyle 1.\qquad\text{(No filter)}

(2.62)

The idea is to estimate the critical value of the filter at the iteration
k
, then select a slightly smaller one (hence “
-0.1
”) guaranteeing the satisfaction of the stability condition.

The optimal approach
which consists in choosing at each iteration the filter by following the following rules:

\displaystyle\text{If }k=0:

\displaystyle K_{k}=\

\displaystyle K_{o},

\displaystyle\text{If }\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k}<1\text{ and }k>0:

\displaystyle K_{k}=\

\displaystyle\text{\eqref{eq:2___36_OptimizationPB_ChoiceK}},

otherwise:

\displaystyle K_{k}=\

\displaystyle 1.\qquad\text{(No filter)}

Simulation results:
The maximal and optimal approaches require that a past iteration exists to allows the estimation of
\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k}
. However, at the first iteration it does not exist. So, one must predefine a filter to use for the first iteration. In this example, and in the rest of this thesis one uses
K_{o}=0.1
. The simulation results of these two approaches are given on Figure
2.8
.

One can see that the optimal approach converges to the optimum of the plant in 2 iterations while finding the optimal value of the filter. The maximal approach is much slower as it requires 10 iterations (or 45 if a very precise estimate of
u_{p}^{\star}
is desired).

Figure 2.8:
Comparison of maximal (in
black
and
blue
) and optimal (in
magenta
) approaches. The blue curve shows what the maximal approach identifies as the limit value of the filter.

Remark on the management of the convergence:
One has noticed that when the iterates
u_{k}
are very close to the solution
u_{p}^{\star}
, the maximal and optimal approaches end up choosing filters
K_{k}
in a quasi-random way. In fact, it is the computation of
\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k}
with (
2.55
) which becomes erratic because the fraction
(u^{*}_{k+1}-u^{*}_{k})/(||u_{k}-u_{k-1}||)
becomes a division of numbers so small that their values is more or less a digital noise related to the precision of the computer. Not managing this can lead to unexpected behaviors. Therefore, one suggests to handle this practical issue with the following improve filter selector:

\displaystyle\text{If }k=0:

\displaystyle K_{k}=K_{o},

\displaystyle\text{If }\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k}<1\ \text{ and }\ k>0\ \text{ et }\ |u_{k}-u_{k-1}|\leq a:

\displaystyle K_{k}=K_{k-1},

\displaystyle\text{If }\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k}<1\ \text{ and }\ k>0\ \text{ et }\ |u_{k}-u_{k-1}|>a:

\displaystyle K_{k}=\eqref{eq:2___gcerbgfj}\text{ or }\text{\eqref{eq:2___36_OptimizationPB_ChoiceK}}

Otherwise:

\displaystyle K_{k}=1,\ \text{(No filter)}.

(2.63)

where

a:=\text{solver's precision}\times 100=10^{-12}\times 100=10^{-10}.

(To obtain the accuracy of the solver one just needs to launch a simulation where instead of optimizing the plant, one optimizes a modified version of the model. One will then see around which values the of iteration size
\|u_{k}-u_{k-1}\|
converge. In this simulation they converge to
\approx 10^{-12}
).

Conclusion

This example shows that contrary to the insight one may have, filtering alone does not necessarily imply a decrease in the speed of convergence. Indeed, it has been shown in a mathematical study that a well-chosen filter
K=0.25
can enable much faster converge than a larger filter
K=0.4
(and, based on several other tests not presented here, this observation is independent of the starting point).

Example 2.2
.

(Is
\bm{K_{k}^{\star}}
still an appropriate choice when
n_{u}
>
1
?)
One considers the following generic RTO problem:

\displaystyle\phi(u,y):=\

\displaystyle y,\

\displaystyle y_{p}=f_{p}(u):=\

\displaystyle\frac{(\bm{u}-\bm{1}_{n_{u}})^{\rm T}\bm{A}(\bm{u}-\bm{1}_{n_{u}})}{2},\

\displaystyle y=f(u):=\

\displaystyle\frac{\bm{u}^{\rm T}\bm{u}}{4},

where
\bm{1}_{n_{u}}:=[1,...,1]^{\rm T}\in\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{u}}
, and where clearly
\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}=\bm{1}_{n_{u}}
. Based on simulation results, the behavior emerging from the use of the filter update strategy (
2.58
)-(
2.63
) is analyzed empirically. In the first part, one considers the case
n_{u}=2
, and in second part one considers the case
n_{u}=3
.

Part 1: “
\bm{n_{u}=2}
”.

One sets:

\bm{A}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}2&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right),

and run a simulation using ISO with the filter update strategy (
2.58
)-(
2.63
). Figure
2.9
shows the simulation results.

Figure 2.9:
Simulation results with the optimal filter adaptation.

One can see that when the value of the filter is close to
1/2
(highlighted with a magenta background), the distance
\|u_{(2)}-u_{p(2)}^{\star}\|
decreases significantly. On the contrary, when it is close to
1/4
(highlighted with a gray background), it is the distance
\|u_{(1)}-u_{p(1)}^{\star}\|
that decreases significantly. Moreover, one can observe that the filter is systematically close to either
1/2
or
1/4
.
These two values
\{1/4,\ 1/2\}
are not meaningless as they correspond to the optimal filter values when working only in the subspaces
\bm{u}=\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}+\alpha[1,0]^{\rm T}
and
\bm{u}=\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}+\alpha[0,1]^{\rm T}
with
\alpha\in\amsmathbb{R}
.

•

In the subspace
\bm{u}=\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}+\alpha[1,0]^{\rm T}
with
\alpha\in\amsmathbb{R}\backslash 0
:

\displaystyle\alpha[1,0]\nabla_{u}\bm{sol}|_{\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}}=1-\frac{\alpha[1,0]\nabla^{2}_{\bm{uu}}\phi_{p}|_{\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}}\alpha[1,0]^{\rm T}}{\alpha[1,0]\nabla^{2}_{\bm{uu}}\phi|_{\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}}\alpha[1,0]^{\rm T}}=1-\frac{2}{1/2}=-3,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\ K^{*}_{k}=\min\left(1,\ \frac{1}{1-(-3)}\right)=\frac{1}{4}.

•

In the subspace
\bm{u}=\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}+\alpha[0,1]^{\rm T}
with
\alpha\in\amsmathbb{R}\backslash 0
:

\displaystyle\alpha[0,1]\nabla_{u}\bm{sol}|_{\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}}=1-\frac{\alpha[0,1]\nabla^{2}_{\bm{uu}}\phi_{p}|_{\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}}\alpha[0,1]^{\rm T}}{\alpha[0,1]\nabla^{2}_{\bm{uu}}\phi|_{\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}}\alpha[0,1]^{\rm T}}=1-\frac{1}{1/2}=-1,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\ K^{*}_{k}=\min\left(1,\ \frac{1}{1-(-1)}\right)=\frac{1}{2}.

Therefore, it seems that the use of the strategy (
2.58
)-(
2.63
) implicitly implies that the solving of the 2-D problem (
n_{u}=2
) is done through a process that transforms this problem into a succession of 1-D problems in subspaces comparable to
\bm{u}=\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}+\alpha[1,0]^{\rm T}
et
\bm{u}=\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}+\alpha[0,1]^{\rm T}
with
\alpha\in\amsmathbb{R}
.

On Figure
2.10
those results are compared with the ones obtained by applying several fixed filters
K=\{0.25,\ 0.33,\ 0.5\}
. The facts are clear, (
2.58
)-(
2.63
) outperforms any fixed filter strategy. Note that the fixed filter
K=0.33
has been selected by hand and is close to the best you can get with a fixed filter for this problem.

Figure 2.10:
The optimal approach (in
black
) is better than any fixed filter approach (in
green
for
K=0.5
,
blue
for
K=0.25
, and
magenta
for
K=0.33
).

(To go further: By varying the values of the elements of the diagonal of
\bm{A}
comparable results are obtained. However, if the matrix
\bm{A}
is not diagonal, i.e. if a rotation is applied to it, then the results will remain similar but the value of
K_{k}^{\star}
will oscillate between the values of the optimal filters in subspaces
\bm{u}=\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}+\alpha\bm{v}_{1}
et
\bm{u}=\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}+\alpha\bm{v}_{2}
, where
\alpha\in\amsmathbb{R}
, and where
(\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2})
are the eigenvectors of
\bm{A}
.)

Part 2: “
\bm{n_{u}=3}
”.

One now considers a 3-D case, and one wants to check whether the empirical observations made on the 2-D problems remain applicable. One sets:

\bm{A}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}2&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&2/3\end{array}\right),

and run a simulation using ISO with the filter update strategy (
2.58
)-(
2.63
). Figure
2.11
shows the simulation results.

Figure 2.11:
Simulation results using the optimal filter adaptation.

The analysis of these results is similar to the analysis done for the 2-D case. Therefore, one can skip the technical details and go directly to the calculation of the optimal filters:

•

In the subspace
\bm{u}=\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}+\alpha[1,0,0]^{\rm T}
with
\alpha\in\amsmathbb{R}\backslash 0
:
K^{*}_{k}=1/4
.

•

In the subspace
\bm{u}=\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}+\alpha[0,1,0]^{\rm T}
with
\alpha\in\amsmathbb{R}\backslash 0
:
K^{*}_{k}=1/2
.

•

In the subspace
\bm{u}=\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}+\alpha[0,0,1]^{\rm T}
with

\alpha\in\amsmathbb{R}\backslash 0
:
K^{*}_{k}=3/4
.

One finds the 3 levels on Figure
2.11
. This validates the extension of the empirical analysis from 2-D cases to higher dimensional cases. Moreover, as for part 1, the strategy (
2.58
)-(
2.63
) is superior to any classical strategy which consists in fixing the filter
K
from the start (see Figure
2.12
). The results obtained with the adaptive filter are in black and those obtained with the fixed filter
K=0.34
(manually identified as the “best” fixed filter) are in magenta.

Conclusion

The filter update strategy (
2.58
)-(
2.63
) leads to the emergence of interesting behaviors when the problem dimension is greater than 1. In such cases, the value of the filter appears to alternate between its optimal values in sub-dimensions of the
n_{u}
-dimensional problem.

(Larger dimensions with matrices
\bm{A}
which are non-diagonal lead to the same observations on testing.)

Figure 2.12:
The adaptive approach (in
black
) is better than any fixed filter approach (whose best results are plotted in
magenta
).

Remark 2.3
.

(Should one use an upper bound for the filter:
K<1
?)

When one uses the strategy (
2.58
)-(
2.63
) it may happen that:

\max\left\{0.1,\frac{1}{1-\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k}}\right\}>1.

(2.64)

This fact calls into question the classical approach (of MA and MAy) of choosing a filter between
0
and
1
. This limit can easily be imposed by replacing (
2.58
) by

\displaystyle K^{\star}_{k}=\

\displaystyle\min\left\{1,\ \max\left\{K_{o},\frac{1}{1-\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k}}\right\}\right\},

\displaystyle\text{si }\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}_{k}^{S}<1.

(2.65)

In the following example, the strategy (
2.58
)-(
2.63
) is compared to the one using an upper bound on the filter (
2.63
)-(
2.65
) on a case where (
2.64
) holds. The results clearly show the superiority of the unbounded approach.

Example 2.3
.

(Comparison of (
2.58
)-(
2.63
) versus (
2.63
)-(
2.65
))

One takes the Example
2.1
and one changes the model
f
:

\displaystyle\phi(u,y):=\

\displaystyle y,\

\displaystyle y_{p}=f_{p}(u):=\

\displaystyle(u-1)^{2},\

\displaystyle y=f(u):=\

\displaystyle 5u^{2}.

This change of model has the effect of changing the functions
sol
associated to this RTO problem which becomes the one represented on the Figure
2.13
.

Figure 2.13:
The function
sol(u_{k})
.

Now, let’s implement ISO with the strategies (
2.58
)-(
2.63
) and (
2.63
)-(
2.65
) and let’s see the results: see Figure
2.14
.

These results show very clearly that the strategy (
2.58
)-(
2.63
) leads to a much faster convergence than (
2.63
)-(
2.65
), in 2 iterations against 111 for a precise convergence and 2 iterations against 25 for an "approximate" convergence. Thus, this simulation supports the idea of dropping the upper bound on the filter
K
.

Figure 2.14:
Results of the strategy (
2.63
)-(
2.65
) (in
black
) and of the strategy (
2.58
)-(
2.63
) (in
magenta
)
.

At this stage one has shown that the equilibrium condition of ISO-D/I can be enforced with the use of locally convex models. One has also shown that the stability can be enforced with the use of a filter on the inputs (
2.51b
) whose value can be automatically tuned with the procedure (
2.63
)-(
2.65
) or (
2.58
)-(
2.63
). Let us now move on to the analysis of the superstability.

2.8
Enforcing the superstability condition

To determine the superstability condition, one must go back to the beginning of the stability analysis. In fact, the only thing one has to do is to start from (
2.51b
) and:

•

replace
\bm{u}_{k+1}^{*}
by (
2.47
);

•

not neglect
term
\mathcal{O}(\|\delta\bm{u}_{k}\|^{2})

to not place the study only in the neighborhood
of
\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}
;

•

add “
-\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}
” on both sides of the equality;

to find:

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{k+1}-\bm{u}^{\star}_{p}=\

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{k}+K(\bm{u}^{\star}_{p}+\nabla^{S}(\delta\bm{u}_{k})(\bm{u}_{k}-\bm{u}_{p}^{\star})+\mathcal{O}(\|\delta\bm{u}_{k}\|^{2})-\bm{u}_{k})-\bm{u}^{\star}_{p},

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\left(1-K\left(1-\nabla^{S}(\delta\bm{u}_{k})-\mathcal{O}(\|\delta\bm{u}_{k}\|)\right)\right)(\bm{u}_{k}-\bm{u}^{\star}_{p}).

(2.66)

This equation is similar to (
2.52
) but the difference is that it takes into account the higher order terms
\mathcal{O}(\|\delta\bm{u}_{k}\|)
of (
2.47
). The superstability condition is then:

-1<1-K\left(1-\left(\nabla^{S}(\delta\bm{u}_{k})+\mathcal{O}(\|\delta\bm{u}_{k}\|)\right)\right)<1,\qquad\forall\delta\bm{u}_{k}\in\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{u}}.

(2.67)

At first glance, the term “
\nabla^{S}(\delta\bm{u}_{k})+\mathcal{O}(\|\delta\bm{u}_{k}\|)
’’ may seem complicated to understand, but in fact it has a simple geometric meaning. It is the ‘‘slope
4
4

4

The tangent of the angle between the line passing through
[\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}{}^{\rm T},\bm{sol}(\bm{u}_{p}^{\star})^{\rm T}]^{\rm T}
and

[(\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}+\delta\bm{u}_{k})^{\rm T},\bm{sol}(\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}+\delta\bm{u}_{k})^{\rm T}]^{\rm T}
and the line passing through
[\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}{}^{\rm T},\bm{0}^{\rm T}]^{\rm T}
and

[(\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}+\delta\bm{u}_{k})^{\rm T},\bm{0}^{\rm T}]^{\rm T}
.

” of the line passing through the points
\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}
and
\bm{sol}(\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}+\delta\bm{u}_{k})
. It is therefore proposed to replace it by a term
\Delta^{S}(\delta\bm{u}_{k})
which is less intimidating and which is defined as follows:

\Delta^{S}(\delta\bm{u}_{k}):=\nabla^{S}(\delta\bm{u}_{k})+\mathcal{O}(||\delta\bm{u}_{k}||)=\left(\frac{\delta\bm{u}_{k}}{\|\delta\bm{u}_{k}\|}\right)^{\rm T}\frac{\bm{sol}(\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}+\delta\bm{u}_{k})-\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}}{\|\delta\bm{u}_{k}\|}

The condition (
2.67
) can then be rewritten more simply as:

\displaystyle 0<K<\frac{2}{1-\hbox to9.73pt{\vbox to10.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.86667pt\lower-5.61665pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.16667pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}

\displaystyle\text{ si: }\hbox to9.73pt{\vbox to10.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.86667pt\lower-4.91666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}

(2.68a)

where

\displaystyle\hbox to9.73pt{\vbox to10.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.86667pt\lower-5.61665pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\displaystyle\underset{\delta\bm{u}_{k}}{\operatorname{min}}\{\Delta^{S}(\delta\bm{u}_{k})\},

\displaystyle\hbox to9.73pt{\vbox to10.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.86667pt\lower-4.91666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.16667pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -6.04 7.08 L 6.04 7.08}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{

\displaystyle\underset{\delta\bm{u}_{k}}{\operatorname{max}}\{\Delta^{S}(\delta\bm{u}_{k})\}.

(2.68b)

As these calculations and reasoning are neither very intuitive, nor easy to visualize, one proposes the following graphic interpretation:

Graphical Interpretation 2.2
.

Let’s take the simplest possible case where
n_{u}=1
and where
sol(u)
is globally
\mathcal{C}^{1}
.
Figure
2.15
shows what the function
sol
could look like (the
black
curve). The green, yellow and red cones shown are the same as the ones used in the graphical interpretation
2.1
. the values of
\hbox to9.73pt{\vbox to10.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.86667pt\lower-5.61665pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.16667pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -6.04 -7.08 L 6.04 -7.08}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{S}
and
\hbox to9.73pt{\vbox to10.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.86667pt\lower-4.91666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.16667pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -6.04 7.08 L 6.04 7.08}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{S}<1
are given graphically as the minimum and maximum slopes between
[u_{p}^{\star},u_{p}^{\star}]
and all the points
[u,sol(u)]
on the curve
sol
.

It should be clear that if all the points
[u,sol(u)]
are in the green cone then superstability is guaranteed without requiring a filter. If all the points
[u,sol(u)]
are in the green and yellow cones then superstability is guaranteed only if the filter is small enough. To understand this at a geometrical level, one must associate Figures
2.5
(image on the right) and
2.15
.

Figure 2.15:
Graphical interpretation of the superstability condition

Unfortunately, it is impossible to guarantee (
2.68
) without questioning what was proposed to guarantee the equilibrium (and thus indirectly the stability). Indeed, forcing the updated model to be convex at any correction point makes the plant’s minimums, maximums and saddle points fixed points of ISO-D/I. In other words, if
\bm{u}_{p}^{\bullet}
is a stationary point – a minimum, maximum or saddle point – of the plant, then
\bm{sol}(\bm{u}_{p}^{\bullet})=\bm{u}_{p}^{\bullet}
. So, if the plant has several stationary points, then it is certain that
\hbox to9.73pt{\vbox to10.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.86667pt\lower-4.91666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.16667pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -6.04 7.08 L 6.04 7.08}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{S}\geq 1
and therefore the condition (
2.68
) cannot be satisfied. Nevertheless, even though the plant maximums and saddle points are fixed points of the improved versions of ISO-D and ISO-I, they are “
unstable
” fixed points. Indeed, the Theorem
2.3
through the equations (
2.45
) shows that the function
\bm{sol}
around such points has directional derivatives
\geq 1
, and one knows that stability is only provided if they are
<1
. So, such points only satisfy the equilibrium conditions, and any iteration close to them, but not equal to them, is likely to lead to a sequence of iterations that diverge, despite the use of local convexifications and the filter. This instability is illustrated in the following example:

Example 2.4
.

Let be an unconstrained RTO problem where
n_{u}=1
, where the cost function is

\displaystyle\phi(u,y):=\

\displaystyle y,

and where the plant and the model are:

\displaystyle y_{p}=f_{p}(u):=\

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{4}u^{2}+\frac{1}{4}u^{3}+\frac{1}{16}u^{4}-\frac{3}{20}u^{5}+\frac{1}{24}u^{6},

\displaystyle\ \ \ y=f(u):=\

\displaystyle\frac{u^{2}}{4}.

Figure
2.16
shows what these curves look like and one can observe that this plant has two minimums (
u_{p}^{\bullet}=\{-1,\ 2\}
), one saddle-point (
u_{p}^{\bullet}=1
) and one maximum (
u_{p}^{\bullet}=0
). Moreover, one can see that the model is convex and therefore satisfies the equilibrium condition.

Figure 2.16:
The cost functions.

The model updated at a point
u_{k}
is:

\phi_{k}(u)=\phi_{p}(u_{k})+\nabla_{u}\phi_{p}|_{u_{k}}(u-u_{k})+\frac{1}{4}(u-u_{k})^{2}.

Figure
2.17
gives the solution
sol(u_{k})
of the optimization problem based on this model updated at
u_{k}
for all
u_{k}
. In addition to this curve, the green, yellow, and red cones of the Graphical Interpretation
2.1
have been added at the four fixed points (where
sol(u_{k})=u_{k}
) so that it is possible to visually judge whether their are stable. Clearly, only the minimums of the plant
u_{p}^{\star}=\{-1,\ 2\}
meet the necessary conditions of stability (
sol(u_{k})
is locally in the green and yellow areas, so there is a filter that stabilizes these points). On the other hand, any point
u_{k}
in the neighborhood of the maximum of the plant
u_{p}^{\bullet}=0
leads to a
u_{k+1}
which goes away from it (because
sol(u_{k})
is locally in the red cone). Concerning the saddle point
u_{p}^{\bullet}=1
one can see that the function
sol
is tangential to the boundary between the red and green cones at this point. On the left side it is tangent but in the green domain while on the right side it is tangent but in the red domain. This means that the points on the left of
u_{p}^{\bullet}=1
would lead to a step towards
u_{p}^{\bullet}=1
, and the points on the right of
u_{p}^{\bullet}=1
would lead to a step away from
u_{p}^{\bullet}=1
.

Figure 2.17:
Graphical analysis of the convergence capabilities on various types of fixed points of the improved versions of ISO-D/I.

So, as one can see with the Example
2.4
, the validity of the condition
\hbox to9.73pt{\vbox to10.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.86667pt\lower-4.91666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.16667pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -6.04 7.08 L 6.04 7.08}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{S}\geq 1
depends on the number of stationary points in the plant and this number cannot be manipulated. If one decides to ignore the condition
\hbox to9.73pt{\vbox to10.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.86667pt\lower-4.91666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.16667pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -6.04 7.08 L 6.04 7.08}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{S}\geq 1
, there remains only the condition:
0<K<2/(1-\hbox to9.73pt{\vbox to10.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.86667pt\lower-5.61665pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.16667pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -6.04 -7.08 L 6.04 -7.08}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{S})
.

The value of
\hbox to9.73pt{\vbox to10.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.86667pt\lower-5.61665pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.16667pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -6.04 -7.08 L 6.04 -7.08}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{S}
is in practice not accessible because a priori neither
\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}
nor
\bm{sol}(\bm{u})
are known,
\forall\bm{u}\in\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{u}}
. However, an idea to estimate
\hbox to9.73pt{\vbox to10.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.86667pt\lower-5.61665pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.16667pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -6.04 -7.08 L 6.04 -7.08}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{S}
could be the following:

\hbox to9.73pt{\vbox to10.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.86667pt\lower-5.61665pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.16667pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -6.04 -7.08 L 6.04 -7.08}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{S}\approx\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\Delta$}}^{S}_{k}:=\underset{\ell=1,...,k}{\operatorname{min}}\left\{\left(\frac{\bm{u}_{\ell}-\bm{u}_{\ell-1}}{\|\bm{u}_{\ell}-\bm{u}_{\ell-1}\|}\right)^{\rm T}\frac{\bm{sol}(\bm{u}_{\ell})-\bm{sol}(\bm{u}_{\ell-1})}{\|\bm{u}_{\ell}-\bm{u}_{\ell-1}\|}\right\}.

(2.69)

Basically,
\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\Delta$}}^{S}_{k}
is the smallest slope observed between two consecutive points until iteration
k
. Of course, there is no guarantee that
\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\Delta$}}^{S}_{k}\leq\hbox to9.73pt{\vbox to10.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.86667pt\lower-5.61665pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.16667pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -6.04 -7.08 L 6.04 -7.08}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{S}
, but selecting the filter in this way can only increase the chances of convergence. So one can choose:

\displaystyle K_{k}=\

\displaystyle\max\left\{0.1,\min\left\{\frac{1}{1-\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k}},\ \frac{2}{1-\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\Delta$}}^{S}_{k}}\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle\text{if: }\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\Delta$}}^{S}_{k}<1\text{ and }\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k}<1,

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\max\left\{0.1,\ \frac{2}{1-\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\Delta$}}^{S}_{k}}\right\}

\displaystyle\text{if: }\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\Delta$}}^{S}_{k}<1.

(2.70)

or

\displaystyle K_{k}=\

\displaystyle\max\left\{0.1,\ \min\left\{1,\ \frac{1}{1-\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k}},\ \frac{2}{1-\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\Delta$}}^{S}_{k}}\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle\text{if: }\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\Delta$}}^{S}_{k}<1\text{ and }\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k}<1,

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\max\left\{0.1,\ \min\left\{1,\ \frac{2}{1-\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\Delta$}}^{S}_{k}}\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle\text{if: }\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\Delta$}}^{S}_{k}<1.

(2.71)

However, doing so is not really necessary because the counterpart associated with this attempt to enforce superstability is a decrease in the positive effects of the adaptive filter illustrated in Example
2.2
. The following example illustrates this last point:

Example 2.5
.

If one restarts from the case
n_{u}=3
of Example
2.2
, and instead of applying the strategy (
2.58
)-(
2.63
), (
2.63
)-(
2.70
) is applied. Then the results of Figure
2.18
are obtained. On the left the magenta curve (

) gives the
K_{k}
obtained with (
2.63
)-(
2.70
) and those obtained with (
2.58
)-(
2.63
) are plotted in black (

). The gray area (

) gives the upper bound for the choice of
K_{k}
, i.e.
2/(1-\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\Delta$}}^{S}_{k})
. The curve on the right gives the evolution of the distance between the iterates
(u_{(1)k},u_{(2)k},u_{(3)k})
and the optimum
\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}
. Unlike in the previous example, the different
u_{(i)k}
are not discriminated since this would not have much interest here.
Clearly, one can see that the addition of the boundary
K_{k}\leq 2/(1-\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\Delta$}}^{S}_{k})
doubles the convergence speed from 15 to 30 iterations.

Figure 2.18:
Strategy (
2.58
)-(
2.63
) vs. Strategy (
2.63
)-(
2.70
).

2.9
Modifier-Filter-Curvature Adaptation

If one applies the set of improvements discussed in the previous three subsections to ISO-D/I, one obtains two new methods that guarantee that the equilibrium and stability conditions are always satisfied. These two methods are called
modifier-filter-curvature adaptation direct (MFCA-D)
and
modifier-filter-curvature Adaptation indirect (MFCA-I)
which are described hereafter.

Alg. 3:
Modifier-Filter-Curvature Adaptation Direct (MFCA-D)

Initialization.
Provide
\bm{u}_{0}
,
\bm{K}_{0}(=0.1)
,
\bm{u}^{*}_{0}(=\bm{u}_{0})
, functions
(\bm{f},\phi,\bm{g})
, and the stopping criterion of step 6).

for

k=0\rightarrow\infty

1)

Measure

(\nabla_{\bm{u}}\phi_{p},\bm{g}_{p},\nabla_{\bm{u}}\bm{g}_{p})|_{\bm{u}_{k}}
on the plant.

2)

Update
the functions
(\phi,\bm{g})
with (
2.18
).

3)

Make
these cost and constraint functions convex at
\bm{u}_{k}
: (
2.37
).

4)

Compute

\bm{u}^{\star}_{k+1}
with (
2.51a
).

5)

Compute
The filter with:

\displaystyle K_{k}:=\

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}K_{o},&\text{if }k=0,\\
K_{k-1}&\text{if }\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k}<1\ \text{ and }\ k>0\ \text{ and }|\bm{u}_{k}-\bm{u}_{k-1}|\leq\bm{a},\\
\max\left\{K_{o},\frac{1}{1-\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.3014pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k}}\right\}&\text{if }\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}^{S}_{k}<1\ \text{ and }\ k>0\ \text{ and }|\bm{u}_{k}-\bm{u}_{k-1}|>\bm{a},\\
1,&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}_{k}^{S}:=\

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\bm{u}_{k}-\bm{u}_{k-1}}{\|\bm{u}_{k}-\bm{u}_{k-1}\|}\right)^{\rm T}\frac{\bm{u}^{\star}_{k+1}-\bm{u}^{\star}_{k}}{\|\bm{u}_{k}-\bm{u}_{k-1}\|}.

(2.76)

6)

Compute

\bm{u}_{k+1}=\bm{u}_{k}+K_{k}(\bm{u}^{*}_{k+1}-\bm{u}_{k})
.

7)

Stop
if
\bm{u}_{k+1}\approx\bm{u}_{k}
and return
\bm{u}_{\infty}:=\bm{u}_{k+1}
.

end

Alg. 4:
Modifier-Filter-Curvature Adaptation Indirect (MFCA-I)

Initialization.
Provide
\bm{u}_{0}
,
\bm{K}_{0}(=0.1)
,
\bm{u}^{*}_{0}(=\bm{u}_{0})
, functions
(\bm{f},\phi,\bm{g})
, and the stopping criterion of step 6).

for

k=0\rightarrow\infty

1)

Measure

(\bm{f}_{p},\nabla_{\bm{u}}\bm{f}_{p})|_{\bm{u}_{k}}
on the plant.

2)

Update
the functions
(\bm{f},\phi,\bm{g})
with (
2.19
).

3)

Make
the cost and constraint functions convex at
\bm{u}_{k}
: (
2.37
).

4)

Compute

\bm{u}^{\star}_{k+1}
with (
2.51a
).

5)

Compute
the filter
K_{k}=\text{\eqref{eq:2___45_LE_FILTRE}}
.

6)

Compute

\bm{u}_{k+1}=\bm{u}_{k}+K_{k}(\bm{u}^{\star}_{k+1}-\bm{u}_{k})
.

7)

Stop
if
\bm{u}_{k+1}\approx\bm{u}_{k}
and return
\bm{u}_{\infty}:=\bm{u}_{k+1}
.

end

Where one can summarize the filter update strategy as follows:

•

If “
k=0
”, i.e. there is no previous iteration that can be used to estimate the gradient of the function
sol
. Then, the selected filter is the
K_{o}
.

•

If “
|\bm{u}_{k}-\bm{u}_{k-1}|\leq a
”, i.e. the size of the last step is less than or equal to the precision of the computer so one cannot estimate the gradient of the function
sol
. Then, the selected filter is the
K_{k-1}
.

•

If “
\utilde{\text{\hskip 0.43057pt$\nabla$}}_{k}^{S}\geq 1
”, i.e. the estimated gradient of the function
sol
is greater than
1
, so one is near a maximum or a saddle point of the plant, so applying a filter is useless. So, no filter needs to applied
K_{k}=1
.

2.10
Conclusion

This chapter starts from the formulation of the theoretical RTO problem. Then, the majority of the fundamental principles and methods of some of the most important contributions to theoretical RTO are rediscovered and improved in all their aspects, through a completely new progressive and didactic re-explanation. Indeed, thanks to a deep an intuitive explanation of how a filter works in the context of RTO, a new very efficient and meaningful filtering protocol has been introduced. However, while this chapter is mainly focused on the ability to converge on plant’s minimums and the speed of this convergence, the next chapter provides answers on how to make this convergence more secure.

Chapter 3
A more secure RTO algorithm

3.1
An observation

The MFCA-D/I methods proposed at the end of the previous chapter are considered and the following thought experiments are performed:

Considering the case illustrated on Figure
3.1
. This is a schematic representation of a 2-D optimization problem whose cost function
\phi_{k}
is convex and whose constraint function
g_{k}
is concave at
\bm{u}_{k}
. Therefore, if MFCA-D/I is applied, then the constraint
g_{k}
would be linearized at
\bm{u}_{k}
to obtain
g^{c}_{k}
. If
g^{c}_{k}
is a relevant approximation of
g_{k}
in the region of
\bm{u}_{k}
, it is clearly not true globally. In fact, a large area of the input space that was predicted to be infeasible becomes feasible. This presents the non-negligible risk that MFCA-D/I selects
\bm{u}_{k+1}^{\star}
and possibly
\bm{u}_{k+1}
in this unfeasible area, as illustrated in Figure
3.1
. This makes it more likely that
\bm{u}_{k+1}
violates the constraints of the plant.

Figure 3.1:
Illustration of the potential risk of convexifying locally concave constraints

3.2
An idea

To counter this problem, an initial option would be to combine the optimization step (
2.51a
) with the filtering step (
2.51b
) in order to enforce
\bm{u}_{k+1}
to be feasible according to the
original
updated model (i.e. the unconvexified one). This idea leads to two new RTO algorithms:

•

Alg
3.2
: MFCA-D with filter-based constraints (KMFCA-D),

•

Alg
3.2
: MFCA-I with filter-based constraints (KMFCA-I).

Alg. 5:
MFCA-D with filter-based constraints (KMFCA-D)

Initialization.
Provide
\bm{u}_{0}
,
\bm{K}_{0}(=0.1)
,
\bm{u}^{*}_{0}(=\bm{u}_{0})
,
\bm{a}
, functions
(\bm{f},\phi,\bm{g})
, and the stopping criterion of step 5).

for

k=0\rightarrow\infty

1)

Measure

(\nabla_{\bm{u}}\phi_{p},\bm{g}_{p},\nabla_{\bm{u}}\bm{g}_{p})|_{\bm{u}_{k}}
on the plant.

2)

Update

\phi
and
\bm{g}
with (
2.18
).

3)

Compute

\phi^{c}_{k}
and
\bm{g}^{c}_{k}
with (
2.37
).

4)

Compute

(\bm{u}^{\star}_{k+1},K_{k},\bm{u}_{k+1})
as follows:

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{\star}_{k+1}:=\

\displaystyle\operatorname{arg}\underset{\bm{u}}{\operatorname{min}}\quad\phi^{c}_{k}(\bm{u}),

\displaystyle\qquad\text{s.t.}\quad\bm{g}^{c}_{k}(\bm{u})\leq\bm{0},

\displaystyle\phantom{\qquad\text{s.t.}\quad}\bm{g}_{k}\big(\bm{u}_{k}+filt_{k}(\bm{u})(\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{k})\big)\leq\bm{0},

(3.1)

\displaystyle K_{k}:=\

\displaystyle filt_{k}(\bm{u}^{\star}_{k+1}),

(3.2)

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{k+1}:=\

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{k}+K_{k}(\bm{u}^{\star}_{k+1}-\bm{u}_{k}),

(3.3)

\displaystyle\text{where}:\qquad\quad

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581ptfilt_{k}(\bm{u}):=\

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{}l}\hskip-5.69054ptK_{o},&\text{if }k=0,\\
\hskip-5.69054ptK_{k-1},&\text{if }nab_{k}(\bm{u})<1,\ k>0\text{ and }|\bm{u}_{k}-\bm{u}_{k-1}|\leq\bm{a}\\
\hskip-5.69054pt\max\left\{K_{o},\frac{1}{1-nab_{k}(\bm{u})}\right\}\hskip-2.84526pt,&\text{if }nab_{k}(\bm{u})<1,\ k>0\text{ and }|\bm{u}_{k}-\bm{u}_{k-1}|>\bm{a},\\
\hskip-5.69054pt1,&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581ptnab_{k}(\bm{u}):=\

\displaystyle\frac{\left(\bm{u}_{k}-\bm{u}_{k-1}\right)^{\rm T}\left(\bm{u}-\bm{u}^{*}_{k}\right)}{\|\bm{u}_{k}-\bm{u}_{k-1}\|^{2}}.

(3.8)

5)

Stop
if
\bm{u}_{k+1}\approx\bm{u}_{k}
and return
\bm{u}_{\infty}:=\bm{u}_{k+1}
.

end

Alg. 6:
MFCA-I with filter-based constraints (KMFCA-I)

Initialization.
Provide
\bm{u}_{0}
,
\bm{K}_{0}(=0.1)
,
\bm{u}^{*}_{0}(=\bm{u}_{0})
,
\bm{a}
, functions
(\bm{f},\phi,\bm{g})
, and the stoping criteron of step 5).

for

k=0\rightarrow\infty

1)

Measure

(\bm{f}_{p},\nabla_{\bm{u}}\bm{f}_{p})|_{\bm{u}_{k}}
on the plant.

2)

Update

\bm{f}
,
\phi
,
\bm{g}
with (
2.19
).

3)

Compute

\phi^{c}_{k}
,
\bm{g}^{c}_{k}
with (
2.37
).

4)

Compute

(\bm{u}^{\star}_{k+1},K_{k},\bm{u}_{k+1})
with (
3.1
), (
3.2
), and (
3.3
).

Stop
if

and return

.
