Computationnal time. It gathers everything ASP does at the end of an experiment from the statistical analysis up to the validation procedure passing by the database compression, the coherency check and the model-based optimisation solving.

6.12
Conclusion

In this chapter improvements are proposed for each of the autopilot functions introduced in chapter
4
except for the signal purifier and the statistical analysis. Most of these improvements are illustrated on a relatively simple example, illustrating in a comprehensible way the functionalities of the new algorithm that has been built. There is indeed only one manipulated variable, one measured perturbation, and one un-measured perturbation. The non-illustrated features are the RTO-plant interface and the use of time as a measured perturbation. However, one believed that these two features are relatively well explained, easy to understand, and what they offer is not particularly subject to debate. Indeed, the RTO-plant interface corresponds to a pre-computation of what the RTO loop would do if it could be computed at a very high speed. So it only brings speed. A preliminary study on a fuel cell has shown that this interface brings significant improvements in the presence of continuous low amplitude disturbances. However, implementing the whole ASP on this case study was not done due to lack of time (this work will certainly appear in a future article together with everything that has been proposed in this chapter).

Chapter 7
Conclusion

7.1
Summary

In this work the challenges related to the monitoring of plants designed to operate at steady-state have been presented and it has been explained how a large number of tasks associated with this monitoring can be automated. One believes to have significantly improved a large part of the state of the art.
In particular, chapters 2, 3 and 5 give clear answers to problems that until then were either unresolved or unidentified, be it the choice of the filtering gain, the model-based feasibility of the decisions that are made, or the correction structure that is used.
In addition, chapters 4 and 6 give a list of functions that need to be fulfilled in order to run a plant efficiently and suggests ways to build these functions and interconnect them.
Many of these functions are completely new such as the ASP’s RTO-plant interface, the experiment designer, the consistency monitor, the compressor, etc. introduced in chapter 6.

However, while this work provides many answers, it also raises many questions and opens research directions in RTO and in other fields related to optimization.

7.2
Potentially interesting research directions

7.2.1
In RTO

Here are seven potentially interesting research directions:

1.

ASP with a correction structures of type A or B. One has started to study this research direction but it turns out that it is a very big piece of work that would probably require a complete PhD to be properly treated. Nevertheless, based on the preliminary study one has done, one can already indicate what the advantages and disadvantages would be of such an autopilot as well as the potential difficulties associated to its design.

Probable advantages:

•

One can expect a better database management. Indeed, the CTP would no longer be defined at the level of the whole plant, but rather at the level of the SMs. Therefore, when the plant’s behavior changes, only the databases of the SMs associated to the SPs whose behaviors have actually changed would be reset, whereas the other databases could be kept.

•

One can expect better corrections. As it has been shown in chapter 5, the correction structures A and B provide better corrections than the D or I ones.

•

It opens the possibility to adapt the correction strategy to the plant-model mismatch of each SMs. Since the model and the plant are split into pieces that are SMs and SPs, one could consider developing methods to identify the local nature of the plant-model mismatch, i.e. at the level of the SMs and SPs. Then, for instance, one could implement parametric corrections where the SMs are only subject to parametric plant-model mismatch.

Likely disadvantages/difficulties:

•

Implementation is clearly one of the main issue of distributed methods (using correction structures of type A of B). Indeed, on the one hand, the structures D and I only require one set of correction functions that are always “plugged” to the model the same way (their inputs are the plant’s inputs, and their outputs are connected to either the plant’s cost and constraints or the plant’s outputs). While on the other hand, the structures A and B are case dependent and may be complex networks of sets of functions that are not always easy to manage when it comes to implement them, i.e. writing the code.

•

The initialization may be less intuitive since Assumptions
6.1
and
6.2
would have be made at the level of each SMs.

•

Computational difficulties may rise from the fact that if one evaluates the uncertainty on the outputs of each SMs, then to evaluate how those uncertainties diffuse and interact through the model may require a lot of computations. For instance, in the section
6.6.2
one shows how the uncertainty on the outputs of the model are affected when they pass through the cost function. Implementing the structures A or B means doing such computations for each SM simultaneously, which is a priori not and easy task.

2.

One could use the measurement of the noise variance
\bm{N}_{y}
to adjust the plant controller. In this work one does not use
\bm{N}_{y}
, see Figure
4.3
. However, when the plant is at steady-state, the quality of two different sets of settings of the controllers could be assessed by their efficiency at rejecting disturbances and measurement noise. Therefore, the better configuration would maintain the outputs of the plant closer to their set-point, i.e. the covariance matrix of the measurement
\bm{N}_{y}
would have smaller eigenvalues. So,
\bm{N}_{y}
could be used as an indicator of the quality of the control structure, and it could be used to maintain or even update it.

3.

The problem of “implicit” constraints. A steady state model of a plant is generally capable of providing a prediction of the steady state of the plant for any input. However, one of the things that has been observed with the TE case study of chapter
5
is that for some tested inputs, the plant does not have a stable state. Either it oscillates or it diverges slowly until it activates an alarm causing the plant to stop. At the moment, no RTO algorithm using SS models (except perhaps fast RTO) can satisfactorily handle such cases. One thinks that it would be important to find a simple and efficient protocol to manage this type of problem via, for example, the addition of a constraint associated to the existence of SS.

4.

Towards a network of ASPs. The sharing of databases of several similar systems could be useful to improve the ASP of each system, identify misunderstood behaviors on a large number of systems (e.g. degradation processes related to the use of equipment over the long term), etc.

5.

The analysis of chapter
5
showed that the correction structures A and B can, thanks to a measurement-based splitting of a model, enable better/deeper corrections. However, this result could also be used to motivate the placement of new sensors on the plant in order to improve its monitoring by enabling better splitting of its subparts.

6.

Sometimes the plant is affected by known but unmeasured disturbances. It could be very interesting to observe them to improve the management of databases (i.e. reset them less often), and to be able to manage their effects in real time with an improved VCL. However, given that the model changes at each iteration and that unknown unmeasured disturbances may be present, the approach to observe them does not seem so obvious. The design of such an observer is an open problem.

7.

The waiting time that is used at the end of each experiment to passively reduce the effects of measurement uncertainty could be chosen together with
\bm{u}_{k+1}
, or even revised in real time. In fact, it is proposed to set this time to a sufficiently large value so that each statistical data on the outputs of the plant is accurate enough. However, nothing says that all experiments must provide accurate results. It is possible that having a rough idea of the output of the plant is enough to motivate a new decision. For instance, if an experiment violates a constraint, then knowing exactly to what extent it is violated may not be of the greatest interest, whereas immediate reaction to bring the plant back to a feasible point might be better. The addition of such a function to ASP (i.e. which would be a revision of the experiment designer) would be interesting since it could accelerate its convergence on
\bm{u}_{p}^{\star}
.

7.2.2
In other research areas

7.2.2.a
Theoretical RTO or Sequential Optimization

One can notice that the mechanism of SQP is very similar to that of the theoretical RTO algorithms that have been built throughout this thesis. We claim that the filter selection mechanism introduced in Chapter 2 can be used to link SQP iterations together via parameter
a^{*}
of (
A.7
) which seems to play a role similar to the one of KMFCaA’s filter. So replacing the merit functions that are usually used to define
a^{*}
by the “simple” filter update strategy of KMFCaA could be, in some particular cases to be defined, a pertinent idea.

7.2.2.b
Theoretical RTO or ROM-Optimization

The problems that theoretical RTO (see section
2.1
) and reduced-order-model based optimization (ROM-optimization) try to solve are almost identical. The only notable difference between these two research areas is of a semantic nature. Indeed, if their objectives are written, their similarities are obvious:

•

The objective of the theoretical RTO is to identify the minimum of a plant using a minimal number of experiments, because each experiment has a cost in terms of time and money that one wants to limit as much as possible. To achieve this goal, an easy-to-evaluate model that can be called a low-fidelity model can be used to guide and improve the exploration of the hard-to-evaluate system that is the plant.

•

ROM-Optimization aims at identifying the minimum of a high-fidelity model by evaluating it in a minimal number of points because each simulation has a cost in terms of time that one wants to limit as much as possible. To achieve this goal, an easy-to-evaluate model that can be called a low-fidelity model can be used to guide and improve the exploration of the hard-to-evaluate system that is the high-fidelity model.

Also, an additional objective of theoretical RTO is to maintain the plant within feasible operation state whereas ROM-Optimization seems to be less focus on this problematic (since its working with simulations and not real processes that can break).

Due to the similarity between these two research areas it is not surprising that the methods developed to solve these problems are similar. It is therefore not surprising that an equivalent of ISO
[
31
]
developed by the RTO community in 2005 had already been proposed by the ROM-Optimization community 7 years earlier in
[
1
]
. This also means that any theoretical RTO method can be applied to ROM-Optimization. It is therefore possible to use KMFCaA to identify the minimum of a high fidelity model.

The concept can even be taken a step further and one can consider optimizing a plant using a high fidelity model.
One could consider the implementation of a double KMFCaA loop to first optimize the high fidelity model by using a low fidelity model easy to evaluate. To then optimize the plant with a KMFA loop, where KMFA corresponds to a KMFCaA method from which one would remove the convexification step. Indeed, to perform this convexification step, one would have to evaluate the Hessian of the high fidelity model. For the same reasons that motivate us not to do it for the plant, one does not wish to do it for the high-fidelity model (requires too many evaluations of functions expensive to evaluate). Figure
7.1
shows what such a structure looks like.

Figure 7.1:
RTO with high- and low-fidelity models. The functions
(\phi^{h}_{k},\bm{g}^{h}_{k},\bm{f}^{h}_{k})
are the functions of the updated high fidelity model
\bm{u}_{k}
on the basis of experiments carried out on the plant. The functions
(\phi^{\ell}_{j,k},\bm{g}^{\ell}_{j,k},\bm{f}^{\ell}_{j,k})
are the functions of the low-fidelity model updated at
\bm{u}_{j}
on the basis of simulations on the high-fidelity model updated at
\bm{u}_{k}
, i.e.
(\phi^{h}_{k},\bm{g}^{h}_{k},\bm{f}^{h}_{k})
.

One might ask what is the point of such a structure when using a simple low fidelity model is (in theory) sufficient to find the optimum of the plant. The interest could be that interposing a high-fidelity model between the low-fidelity model and the plant could reduce the number of experiments to be performed by making them more relevant. Indeed, the evaluations of the high-fidelity model and the plant are time-consuming, but only the evaluations of the plant can entail costs and real risks.

Appendix A
Technical complements to Chapter 2

A.1
Correction of a composed function

Let there be three
\mathcal{C}^{2}
functions:

\displaystyle\phi:\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{u}},\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{y}}\rightarrow\

\displaystyle\amsmathbb{R},

\displaystyle\bm{f}_{p}:\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{u}}\rightarrow\

\displaystyle\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{y}},

\displaystyle\bm{f}_{k+1}:\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{u}}\rightarrow\

\displaystyle\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{y}},

and let’s state that at the point
\bm{u}_{k}\in\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{u}}
the function
\bm{f}_{k+1}
is:

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\bm{y}_{k}:=\bm{f}_{k+1}|_{\bm{u}_{k}}:=\

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\bm{f}_{p}|_{\bm{u}_{k}}:=\bm{y}_{p,k},

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\nabla_{\bm{u}}\bm{f}_{k+1}|_{\bm{u}_{k}}:=\

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\nabla_{\bm{u}}\bm{f}_{p}|_{\bm{u}_{k}},

(A.1)

where the variables
\bm{y}_{k}
and
\bm{y}_{p,k}
are introduced to make the following developments more readable. Then, one can observe that the composed functions
\phi\circ\bm{f}_{p}
and
\phi\circ\bm{f}_{k+1}
have similar Taylor series expansions around
\bm{u}_{k}
:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{}c@{}c@{}l}\phi(\bm{u},\bm{f}_{k+1}(\bm{u}))=&\ \phi|_{\bm{u}_{k},\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\bm{y}_{k}}&\ +&\ \big(\partial_{\bm{u}}\phi|_{\bm{u}_{k},\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\bm{y}_{k}}+\partial_{\bm{y}}\phi|_{\bm{u}_{k},\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\bm{y}_{k}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\nabla_{\bm{u}}\bm{f}_{k+1}|_{\bm{u}_{k}}\big)\left(\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{k}\right)&\ +\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}h.o.t.,\\
\phi(\bm{u},\bm{f}_{p}(\bm{u}))=&\ \phi|_{\bm{u}_{k},\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\bm{y}_{p,k}}&\ +&\ \big(\partial_{\bm{u}}\phi|_{\bm{u}_{k},\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\bm{y}_{p,k}}+\partial_{\bm{y}}\phi|_{\bm{u}_{k},\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\bm{y}_{p,k}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\nabla_{\bm{u}}\bm{f}_{p}|_{\bm{u}_{k}}\big)\left(\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{k}\right)&\ +\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}h.o.t.,\\
\end{array}

where the effects of the definitions (
A.1
) are illustrated through the correspondence of the color code used (green and blue). If differences exist between
\phi\circ\bm{f}_{p}
and
\phi\circ\bm{f}_{k+1}
, then they reside in the higher order terms of this development (“
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}h.o.t.
” – where the color red is used to mark a source of the differences). It goes without saying that if the functions
\bm{f}_{k+1}
and
\bm{f}_{p}
are affine in
\bm{u}
and
\bm{y}
, then
\bm{f}_{k+1}=\bm{f}_{p}
and
(\phi\circ\bm{f}_{p})=(\phi\circ\bm{f}_{k+1})
.

Remark A.1
.

(The deep effects of the indirect approach)

One can write the Hessian of
(\phi\circ\bm{f}_{k+1})
at
\bm{u}_{k}
in the following way:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{uu}}(\phi\circ\bm{f}_{k+1})|_{\bm{u_{k}},\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\bm{y}_{k}}=...

\displaystyle\qquad\Big[\partial_{\bm{u}}\phi(\partial_{\bm{u}}\phi)^{\rm T}+2\partial_{\bm{uy}}\phi\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\nabla_{\bm{u}}\bm{f}_{k+1}+(\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\nabla_{\bm{u}}\bm{f}_{k+1})^{\rm T}\partial_{\bm{yy}}\phi\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\nabla_{\bm{u}}\bm{f}_{k+1}+...

\displaystyle\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{n_{y}}\left[\partial_{y_{(i)}}\phi\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\bm{f}_{k+1}\right]\Big]\Big|_{\bm{u}_{k},\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\bm{y}_{k}}.

In the case of direct approaches, such that the equalities (
A.1
) are not guaranteed, all green and blue terms in this equation are wrong. Specifically, several things could be sources of error in the estimation of
(\nabla_{\bm{uu}}(\phi\circ\bm{f}_{p})|_{\bm{u_{k}},\bm{y}_{k}})
with
(\nabla_{\bm{uu}}(\phi\circ\bm{f}_{k+1})|_{\bm{u_{k}},\bm{y}_{k}})
:

•

The functions
\nabla_{\bm{u}}\bm{f}_{k+1}
would be structurally wrong, therefore
a priori

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\nabla_{\bm{u}}\bm{f}_{k+1}\neq\nabla_{\bm{u}}\bm{f}_{p},\qquad\forall\bm{u}\in\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{u}}

with a few potential exceptions.

•

The derivatives and Hessians of the cost function, although structurally correct, would not be evaluated at the right point if
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{y}_{k}\neq\bm{y}_{p,k}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{\bm{uu}}\phi|_{\bm{u}_{k},\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{y}_{k}}\neq\

\displaystyle\partial_{\bm{uu}}\phi|_{\bm{u}_{k},\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{y}_{p,k}},

\displaystyle\partial_{\bm{uy}}\phi|_{\bm{u}_{k},\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{y}_{k}}\neq\

\displaystyle\partial_{\bm{uy}}\phi|_{\bm{u}_{k},\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{y}_{p,k}},

\displaystyle\partial_{\bm{yy}}\phi|_{\bm{u}_{k},\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{y}_{k}}\neq\

\displaystyle\partial_{\bm{yy}}\phi|_{\bm{u}_{k},\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{y}_{p,k}},

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{(i)}}\phi|_{\bm{u}_{k},\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{y}_{k}}\neq\

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{(i)}}\phi|_{\bm{u}_{k},\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\bm{y}_{p,k}},\ \forall i=1,...,n_{y}.

It is therefore clear that choosing an indirect approach that brings the equalities (
A.1
) allows to partially correct the high order terms (“
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}h.o.t.
”). However, it is less clear that these partial corrections ultimately provide a real benefit. A realistic case study (the WO reactor) and a statistical mathematical analysis show that a slight improvement can be expected
[
58
]
.

A.2
Solution of the quadratic optimization problem

A.2.1
Solving a quadratic problem with
equality
constraints

Consider a quadratic optimization problem with equality constraints:

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{k+1}=\operatorname{arg}\underset{\bm{u}}{\operatorname{min}}\quad

\displaystyle q_{0}+\bm{q}^{\rm T}\bm{u}+\frac{1}{2}\bm{u}^{\rm T}\bm{Q}\bm{u},

s.t.

\displaystyle\bm{h}^{a}+\bm{H}^{a}\bm{u}=\bm{0}.

(A.2)

This problem has an analytical solution which is (see
[
57
]
page 451):

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\bm{u}_{k+1}\\
\bm{\lambda}\end{array}\right)=-\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\bm{Q}&\bm{H}^{a\rm T}\\
\bm{H}^{a}&\bm{0}\end{array}\right)^{-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\bm{q}\\
\bm{h}^{a}\end{array}\right),

(A.3)

where
\bm{\lambda}
is the vector of Lagrange multipliers associated with each of the equality constraints of (
A.2
). Starting from this result, let’s see how one can solve the cases where the constraints are inequalities instead of equalities.

A.2.2
Solving a quadratic problem with
inequality
constraints

Consider a quadratic optimization problem with inequality constraints:

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{k+1}=\operatorname{arg}\underset{\bm{u}}{\operatorname{min}}\quad

\displaystyle q_{0}+\bm{q}^{\rm T}\bm{u}+\frac{1}{2}\bm{u}^{\rm T}\bm{Q}\bm{u},

s.t.

\displaystyle\bm{g}(\bm{u})=\bm{h}+\bm{H}\bm{u}\leq\bm{0}.

(A.4)

One idea to solve this problem is to identify the constraints that are active at
\bm{u}_{k+1}
to replace the inequality constraints of (
A.4
) by equality constraints on, and only on, these active constraints.

This work of identifying active constraints can be done in the following way:

(Step 0 – Initialization
) First, a point
\bm{u}_{0}^{test}
must be chosen. Then, the active constraints at
\bm{u}_{0}^{test}
are identified and used to define a set of active constraints:
\mathcal{A}^{test}
. Then one enters in a cycle of update of this set
\mathcal{A}^{test}
. One initializes
j=0
and goes to Step 1:

(Step 1 – Computation of
\bm{u}^{test}
)
Consider a quadratic optimization problem with inequality constraints:

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{j+1}^{test}=\operatorname{arg}\underset{\bm{u}}{\operatorname{min}}\quad

\displaystyle q_{0}+\bm{q}^{\rm T}\bm{u}+\frac{1}{2}\bm{u}^{\rm T}\bm{Q}\bm{u},

s.t.

\displaystyle\bm{g}^{test}(\bm{u})=\bm{h}^{test}+\bm{H}^{test}\bm{u}=\bm{0},

(A.5)

where
\bm{g}^{test}=[g_{(i)}]_{i\in\mathcal{A}^{test}}
a vector concatenating the constraints that one believes are active at
\bm{u}_{k+1}
. The solution of this problem is "easy" to calculate with the analytical solution (
A.3
).

(Step 2 – Test 1)
Check if at the point
\bm{u}_{j+1}^{test}
the set of constraints
\bm{g}\leq 0
, i.e. not only
\bm{g}^{test}
. If this test is passed, then the constraints that are active at
\bm{u}_{k+1}
are part of
\mathcal{A}^{test}
and one can move on to Step 3. If this is not the case, then at the point
\bm{u}^{test}
one violates a constraint that was supposedly inactive according to
\bm{u}_{j}^{test}
. One will therefore explore the segment linking
\bm{u}_{j}^{test}
to
\bm{u}_{j+1}^{test}
to find the closest point to
\bm{u}_{j+1}^{test}
that does not violate the constraints. Then, one takes the active constraints at this point to redefine
\mathcal{A}^{test}
, increments the counter
j
, and returns to Step 1.

(Step 3 – Test 2)
If one reaches this step it is that a priori
\mathcal{A}^{test}
contains
enough
constraints so that
\bm{u}_{j+1}^{test}
is feasible. Now let’s check if
\mathcal{A}^{test}
contains
too many
constraints. To carry out this verification, it is sufficient to check whether the vector of Lagrange multipliers calculated at the same time as
\bm{u}_{j+1}^{test}
with (
A.3
) are all
\geq 0
. If this is not the case, then one must remove from
\mathcal{A}^{test}
all constraints associated with Lagrange multipliers that are
<0
, increment the counter
j
, and return to Step 1. Otherwise, one moves to step 4.

(Step 4 – Finalization)
If both tests are passed, then
\bm{u}^{test}
is the solution we are looking for, i.e.
\bm{u}_{j+1}^{test}=\bm{u}_{k+1}
.

Finally, the real challenge associated with solving a quadratic problem with inequality constraints is the identification of the active constraints.

Remark A.2
.

(Risk of constraint violation part 1)
It is clear that in order to identify these active constraints one has to implement a strategy that may involve the repetitive and/or frequent violation of constraints, especially during Step 2 with the exploration of the segment
[\bm{u}_{j}^{test},\bm{u}_{j+1}^{test}]
.

On the basis of this strategy, let us introduce the operating principles of the SQP (sequential quadratic programming) nonlinear optimization problem solver.

A.2.3
Solving a non-linear optimization problems (SQP method)

Consider a nonlinear optimization problem with inequality constraints:

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{k+1}=\operatorname{arg}\underset{\bm{u}}{\operatorname{min}}\quad\phi(\bm{u}),\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\bm{g}(\bm{u})\leq\bm{0}.

(A.6)

One idea to solve this problem would be to choose a candidate
\bm{u}_{0}^{*}
that one hopes to be close to
\bm{u}_{k+1}
, and to repeat
\forall\ell\rightarrow\infty
the following steps:

•

(Step A)
Replace the functions
\phi
and
\bm{g}
by quadratic and linear approximations of themselves at
\bm{u}_{\ell}^{*}
,

•

(Step B)
Solve the quadratic problem with inequality constraints based on these approximations as seen in the previous section to find
\bm{u}_{\ell+1}

•

(Step C)
Select a scalar
a^{*}
so that the functions
\phi
and
\bm{g}
are significantly “better” at

\bm{u}_{\ell+1}^{*}=\bm{u}_{\ell}^{*}+a^{*}(\bm{u}_{\ell+1}-\bm{u}_{\ell}^{*})

(A.7)

than at
\bm{u}_{\ell}^{*}
. If
\bm{u}_{\ell+1}^{*}=\bm{u}_{\ell}^{*}
stop the procedure and return
\bm{u}_{k+1}=\bm{u}_{\ell}^{*}
. Otherwise, increment
\ell
, and return to
Step A
.

The challenge of SQP is to ensure (i) progress towards the desired solution
\bm{u}_{k+1}
at each iteration, (ii) that the convergence on
\bm{u}_{k+1}
will be observed in a limited time (or limited number of iterations), as well as (iii) the set of challenges associated with solving a quadratic problem with inequality constraints.

Remark A.3
.

(Risk of constraint violation part 2)
As at each step SQP uses an approximation of the NLP to progress. The intermediate points (
\bm{u}_{\ell}^{*}
) only satisfies the
approximated
constraints of this NLP, and this satisfaction obviously does not imply the satisfaction of the
actual
constraints. Constraints violations can then be observed during these intermediate steps and/or during the choice of
a^{*}
.

The Remarks
A.2
and
A.3
show that a solver like SQP presents risks of constraint violation at different levels of its exploration strategy towards the optimum of an optimization problem. Moreover, at each Step A of SQP, one has to obtain the Hessian of the cost function of the problem one wants to solve. This is in addition to the potentially large number of evaluations required for the calculation of
a^{*}
and for the convergence on
\bm{u}_{k+1}
, which ultimately results in SQP being not necessarily implementable (as such) on a real plant.

A.3
Proof of Theorem
2.3

A.3.1
Statement A: Global shape of
sol

Let’s rewrite the optimization problem (
2.51a
) in the following way:

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{*}_{k+1}:=\bm{sol}(\bm{v}_{k})=\operatorname{arg}\underset{\bm{u}}{\operatorname{min}}\quad

\displaystyle\phi_{k}(\bm{u}):=\widetilde{g}_{(0)}(\bm{u},\bm{v}_{k}),

(A.8)

s.t.

\displaystyle\bm{g}_{k}(\bm{u}):=\widetilde{\bm{g}}(\bm{u},\bm{v}_{k})\leq\bm{0}.

where the variable
\bm{v}\in\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{u}}
is introduced as a copy of
\bm{u}
indicating the point at which the model is updated. The functions of this problem can be approximated around
(\bm{u}^{*}_{k+1},\bm{v}_{k})
via the following Taylor series
\forall i=0,...,n_{g}
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{(i)}(\bm{u},\bm{v})=\

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{*}+\partial_{\bm{v}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{*}^{\rm T}(\bm{v}-\bm{v}_{k})+\partial_{\bm{u}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{*}^{\rm T}(\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{k+1}^{*})+...

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\bm{v}-\bm{v}_{k})^{\rm T}\partial_{\bm{vv}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{*}(\bm{v}-\bm{v}_{k})+\frac{1}{2}(\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{k+1}^{*})^{\rm T}\partial_{\bm{uu}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{*}(\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{k+1}^{*})+...

\displaystyle(\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{k+1}^{*})^{\rm T}\partial_{\bm{uv}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{*}(\bm{v}-\bm{v}_{k})+h.o.t..

Which can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{(i)}(\bm{u},\bm{v})=\

\displaystyle\big[a_{i}+\bm{b}_{i}^{\rm T}\bm{v}+\frac{1}{2}\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{C}_{i}\bm{v}\big]+\big[\bm{d}_{i}^{\rm T}+\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{E}_{i}\big]\bm{u}+\frac{1}{2}\bm{u}^{\rm T}\bm{F}_{i}\bm{u}.

(A.9)

where the details are

\displaystyle a_{i}:=\

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{*}-\partial_{\bm{v}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{*}^{\rm T}\bm{v}_{k}-\partial_{\bm{u}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{*}^{\rm T}\bm{u}_{k+1}^{*}+\frac{1}{2}\bm{v}_{k}^{\rm T}\partial_{\bm{vv}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{*}\bm{v}_{k}+...

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\bm{u}_{k+1}^{*})^{\rm T}\partial_{\bm{uu}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{*}\bm{u}_{k+1}^{*}+(\bm{u}_{k+1}^{*})^{\rm T}\partial_{\bm{uv}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{*}\bm{v}_{k},

\displaystyle\bm{b}_{i}:=\

\displaystyle\partial_{\bm{v}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{*}^{\rm T}-\partial_{\bm{vv}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{*}\bm{v}_{k}-\partial_{\bm{uv}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{*}\bm{u}_{k+1}^{*},

\displaystyle\bm{C}_{i}:=\

\displaystyle\partial_{\bm{vv}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{*},

\displaystyle\bm{d}_{i}:=\

\displaystyle\partial_{\bm{u}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{*}-\partial_{\bm{uu}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{*}\bm{u}_{k+1}^{*}-\partial_{\bm{uv}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{*}\bm{v}_{k},

\displaystyle\bm{E}_{i}:=\

\displaystyle\partial_{\bm{vu}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{*},

\displaystyle\bm{F}_{i}:=\

\displaystyle\partial_{\bm{uu}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{*}.

If one replaces the functions of (
A.8
) by their convex approximations (
A.9
), then one obtains the following multi-parametric quadratic program (mp-QP):

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{*}=\bm{sol}(\bm{v})=\operatorname{arg}\

\displaystyle\underset{\bm{u}}{\operatorname{min}}\quad\big[\bm{d}_{0}^{\rm T}+\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{E}_{0}\big]\bm{u}+\frac{1}{2}\bm{u}^{\rm T}\bm{F}_{0}\bm{u}

(A.10)

s.t.

\displaystyle\big[a_{i}+\bm{b}_{i}^{\rm T}\bm{v}+\frac{1}{2}\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{C}_{i}\bm{v}\big]+\big[\bm{d}_{i}^{\rm T}+\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{E}_{i}\big]\bm{u}=\bm{0},\quad\forall i\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{v}),

where
\mathcal{A}(\bm{v})
is the subset of the constraints of (
A.10
) that are active at
\bm{u}^{*}
when the model is updated at
\bm{v}
. By definition, (
A.10
) correctly approximates the solution of (
A.8
) when
\bm{v}
is close to
\bm{v}_{k}
. The explicit solution of this problem is (according to (
A.3
)):

-\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\bm{F}_{0}&\bm{DE}_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{v})}^{\rm T}\\
\bm{DE}_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{v})}&\bm{0}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\bm{sol}(\bm{v})\\
\bm{\lambda}(\bm{v})\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}(\bm{d}_{0}^{\rm T}+\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{E}_{0})\\
\bm{A}_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{v})}\end{array}\right)

(A.11)

where:

•

\bm{\lambda}(\bm{v})
is a function that returns the Lagrange multiplier vector of the active constraints at the minimum of the model updated at
\bm{v}
,

•

\bm{DE}_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{v})}:=[\bm{d}_{i}^{\rm T}+\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{E}_{i}]_{\forall i\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{v})},
and

\bm{A}_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{v})}:=\big[a_{i}+\bm{b}_{i}^{\rm T}\bm{v}+\frac{1}{2}\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{C}_{i}\bm{v}\big]_{\forall i\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{v})}
, where
[(\cdot)_{i}]_{\forall i\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{v})}
is the ordered concatenation of
(\cdot)_{i}
whose
i\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{v})
. For example, if only the constraints
i=1,2,4
are active at
\bm{u}^{*}
then:

\bm{DE}_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{v})}=[\bm{d}_{i}^{\rm T}+\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{E}_{i}]_{\forall i\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{v})}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\bm{d}_{1}^{\rm T}+\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{E}_{1}\\
\bm{d}_{2}^{\rm T}+\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{E}_{2}\\
\bm{d}_{4}^{\rm T}+\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{E}_{4}\end{array}\right).

What one can learn from (
A.11
) is that
\bm{sol}
and
\bm{\lambda}
are functions of
\bm{v}
which are globally continuous (
\mathcal{C}^{0}
) and at least piecewise-
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, where each piece is defined by a different set of active constraints
\mathcal{A}(\bm{v})
.
This result is illustrated in the following example:

Example A.1
.

One considers the following RTO problem:

\displaystyle\phi(u,y):=\

\displaystyle y,\

\displaystyle y_{p}=f_{p}(u):=\

\displaystyle\frac{(u-1)^{2}}{3}+\frac{(u-1)^{4}}{60},\

\displaystyle y=f(u):=\

\displaystyle\frac{u^{2}}{4}.

Subject to the following two cases of constraints:

Scenario 1
:
-5\leq u\leq 5
, i.e.:

\displaystyle g_{(1)}=-5-u,

\displaystyle g_{(2)}=u-5,

Scenario 2
:
\phantom{-}1\leq u\leq 5
, i.e.:

\displaystyle g_{(1)}=\phantom{-}1-u,

\displaystyle g_{(2)}=u-5.

If one implements ISO-D/I, then the modified optimization problem at a point
v
is according to the notations of (
A.8
):

\displaystyle sol(v)=\operatorname{arg}\underset{u}{\operatorname{min}}\quad

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{(0)}(u,v),

s.t.

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{g}}(u,v)\leq\bm{0}.

where:

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{(0)}(u,v):=\

\displaystyle\frac{(v-1)^{2}}{3}+\frac{(v-1)^{4}}{60}+\left(\frac{2(v-1)}{3}+\frac{(v-1)^{3}}{15}\right)u+\frac{u^{2}}{2},

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{(1)}(u,v):=\

\displaystyle g_{(1)}(u),

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{(2)}(u,v):=\

\displaystyle g_{(2)}(u).

One will not try to explicitly compute
sol(v)
or the values of the Lagrange multipliers
\lambda_{(1)}(v)
and
\lambda_{(2)}(v)
associated to the constraints
\widetilde{g}_{(1)}
and
\widetilde{g}_{(2)}
. Instead one uses a solver to evaluate their values at a large number of points. The results obtained for each scenario are shown in Figure
A.1
. These results confirm and illustrate that the functions
(sol,\lambda_{(i)}\ \forall i=1,...,n_{g})
are globally
\mathcal{C}^{0}
and at least piecewise-
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, where each piece is associated with a different set of active constraints. Scenario 1 shows a case where these functions are at least
\mathcal{C}^{1}
at
v=u_{p}^{\star}=1
and the Scenario 2 shows a case where these functions are only
\mathcal{C}^{0}
at
v=u_{p}^{\star}=1
.

a) Scenario 1

b) Scenario 2

Figure A.1:
The functions
sol
,
\lambda_{(1)}
and
\lambda_{(2)}
are globally
\mathcal{C}^{0}
and at least piecewise-
\mathcal{C}^{1}
. Each “piece” is distinguished by a a color: respectively
\bm{\mathcal{A}(v)=\varnothing}
,
\bm{\mathcal{A}(v)=1}
, and
\bm{\mathcal{A}(v)=2}
.

A.3.2
Statements B and C:
sol
in the vicinity of the plant’s stationary points

If a point
(\bm{u}^{\bullet},\bm{\lambda}_{p})
is a KKT point of the plant, then the derivative of the Lagrangian of the plant
\mathcal{L}_{p}(\bm{u})=\sum_{i=0}^{n_{g}}\lambda_{p(i)}g_{p(i)}|_{\bm{u}^{\bullet}},
satisfies:

\nabla_{\bm{u}}\mathcal{L}_{p}(\bm{u})=\sum_{i=0}^{n_{g}}\lambda_{p(i)}\nabla_{\bm{u}}g_{p(i)}|_{\bm{u}^{\bullet}}=0.

(A.12)

In addition, if
(\bm{u}^{\bullet},\bm{\lambda}_{p})
is a KKT point of the plant then it is also a KKT point of the model modified with ISO-D or -I, or any other method satisfying the applicability condition of Theorem
2.2
. So:

\displaystyle\bm{sol}(\bm{v}^{\bullet})=\

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{\bullet},

\displaystyle\bm{\lambda}(\bm{v}^{\bullet})=\bm{\lambda}_{p}.

(A.13)

Now, let’s analyze how
\bm{sol}
behaves in the neighborhood of
\bm{v}^{\bullet}
. To do this, one approximates the Lagrangian of the model around this stationary point in the following way:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\ }l}\mathcal{L}(\bm{u},\bm{v})&:=\sum_{i=0}^{n_{g}}\lambda_{(i)}|_{\bm{v}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{\bm{u},\bm{v}},\\
\widetilde{g}_{(i)}(\bm{u},\bm{v})&:=g_{p(i)}|_{\bm{v}}+\nabla_{\bm{v}}g_{p(i)}|_{\bm{v}}^{\rm T}(\bm{u}-\bm{v})+\frac{1}{2}(\bm{u}-\bm{v})^{\rm T}\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{\bm{v},\bm{v}}(\bm{u}-\bm{v}),\\
\lambda_{(0)}(\bm{v})&:=1.\end{array}

Indeed, since the correction point and the minimum of the updated model are the same, the 0th and 1st order terms of the functions
\widetilde{g}_{(i)}
are those of the plant evaluated at the correction point while the 2nd order term is the Hessian of the updated model evaluated at
\bm{v}^{\bullet}
. Of course, this approximation is only valid for
\forall\bm{v},\bm{u}\in\mathcal{B}(\bm{u}^{\bullet},r\rightarrow 0)
.

The functions
\bm{sol}(\bm{v})
and
\bm{\lambda}(\bm{v})
can be linked within the same expression in the following way:

\partial_{\bm{u}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{sol}(\bm{v}),\bm{v})=\sum_{i=0}^{n_{g}}\lambda_{(i)}|_{\bm{v}}\left(\nabla_{\bm{v}}g_{p(i)}|_{\bm{v}}+\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{\bm{v},\bm{v}}(\bm{sol}(\bm{v})-\bm{v})\right)=\bm{0}

(A.14)

By insulating
\bm{sol}(\bm{v})
on the left side of the equation one finds:

\displaystyle\bm{sol}(\bm{v})=\

\displaystyle\bm{v}-\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n_{g}}\lambda_{(i)}|_{\bm{v}}\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{\bm{v},\bm{v}}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n_{g}}\lambda_{(i)}|_{\bm{v}}\nabla_{\bm{v}}g_{p(i)}|_{\bm{v}}\right).

(A.15)

(One notices that one can recognize that
\bm{sol}(\bm{v}^{\bullet})=\bm{v}^{\bullet}
because for
\bm{v}=\bm{v}^{\bullet}
there is a
\bm{\lambda}_{p}\in\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{g}+}
such that (
A.12
) holds.)
From this expression one finds that:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{sol}(\bm{v})|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}=\

\displaystyle\bm{I}-\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n_{g}}\lambda_{(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\widetilde{g}_{(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet},\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\Big)^{-1}...

\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{n_{g}}\lambda_{(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\nabla_{\bm{vv}}g_{p(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}+\nabla_{\bm{v}}g_{p(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\lambda_{(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}^{\rm T}\Big),

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\bm{I}-\left(\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\mathcal{L}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet},\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\right)^{-1}\nabla_{\bm{vv}}\mathcal{L}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}-\left(\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\mathcal{L}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet},\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\right)^{-1}\sum_{i=0}^{n_{g}}\nabla_{\bm{v}}g_{p(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\lambda_{(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}^{\rm T}

(A.16)

Let’s focus on the term:

\left(\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\mathcal{L}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet},\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\right)^{-1}\sum_{i=0}^{n_{g}}\nabla_{\bm{v}}g_{p(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\lambda_{(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}^{\rm T}.

(A.17)

which can be simplified in the following three ways:

•

Concerning the cost function (
i=0
):
Starting from the definition of
\lambda_{(0)}
:

\displaystyle\forall\bm{v}\in\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{u}},\ \lambda_{(0)}|_{\bm{v}}:=\ 1,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\ \

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{v}}\lambda_{(0)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}=\bm{0},

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\ \

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{v}}g_{p(0)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\lambda_{(0)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}^{\rm T}=\bm{0}.

(A.18)

•

Concerning the
inactive
constraints (
i\not\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{v}^{\bullet})
):

For all constraints that are not active at
\bm{v}^{\bullet}
, i.e.
\forall i\not\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{v}^{\bullet})
, it is clear that:

\displaystyle g_{p(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}<0,\ \&\ g_{p(i)}\in\mathcal{C}^{0}\quad\Rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\forall\bm{v}\in\mathcal{B}(\bm{v}^{\bullet},r\rightarrow 0):\ g_{p(i)}|_{\bm{v}}<0,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\forall\bm{v}\in\mathcal{B}(\bm{v}^{\bullet},r\rightarrow 0):\ \lambda_{(i)}|_{\bm{v}}=0,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\forall i\not\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{v}^{\bullet}):\ \nabla_{\bm{v}}\lambda_{(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}=\bm{0},

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{i\not\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{v}^{\bullet})}\nabla_{\bm{v}}g_{p(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\lambda_{(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}^{\rm T}=\bm{0}.

(A.19)

•

Concerning the
weakly active
constraints according to the direction
\delta\bm{v}
(
i\not\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{v}^{\bullet},\delta\bm{v})
):
One says that a constraint
i
is weakly active in a direction
\delta\bm{v}
if:

\displaystyle g_{p(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}=\

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}h\rightarrow 0\\
h>0\end{subarray}}{\operatorname{lim}}\ g_{p(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}+h\delta\bm{v}}<\

\displaystyle 0.

If this is the case, as
\forall h
close to
0
and greater than
0
one has
g_{p(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}+h\delta\bm{v}}<0
, it is then clear that
\lambda_{(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}+h\delta\bm{v}}=0
. And since
\lambda_{(i)}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{0}
function of
\bm{v}
:

\displaystyle\lambda_{(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}=\

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{v}}\lambda_{(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\delta\bm{v}=\

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\text{if $i\not\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{v}^{\bullet},\delta\bm{v})$}.

(A.20)

From these three observations one can reduce (
A.17
) to:

\text{\eqref{eq:A___8_rbhuceeeeegc}}=\left(\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\mathcal{L}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet},\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\right)^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{v}^{\bullet},\delta\bm{v})}\nabla_{\bm{v}}g_{p(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\lambda_{(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}^{\rm T}.

(A.21)

So only the active constraints at
\bm{v}^{\bullet}
remain active when one moves in the direction of
\delta\bm{v}
(which is linked to
\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{sol}
). By definition the constraints are such that:

\displaystyle\bm{g}^{a}(\bm{sol}(\bm{v}))=\

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}(\bm{sol}(\bm{v}))=\

\displaystyle 0,

(A.22)

where

\displaystyle\bm{g}^{a}_{p}:=\

\displaystyle[g_{p(i)}]_{i\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{v}^{\bullet},\delta\bm{v})}.

One approximates them around
\bm{v}^{\bullet}
with a Taylor series:

\displaystyle\bm{g}^{a}(\bm{sol}(\bm{v}))=\

\displaystyle\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}+\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}(\bm{sol}(\bm{v})-\bm{v})|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}},

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\ \ \nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}(\bm{sol}(\bm{v}))=\

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}+\nabla_{\bm{vv}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}(\bm{sol}(\bm{v})-\bm{v})+\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}(\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{sol}(\bm{v})-\bm{I})^{\rm T},

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}+\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}(\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{sol}(\bm{v})-\bm{I})^{\rm T},

(A.23)

One combines (
A.22
) and (
A.23
):

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}(\bm{I}-\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{sol}(\bm{v}))^{\rm T}=\

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}},

One multiplies on each side by
\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}^{\rm T}\left(\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}^{\rm T}\right)^{-1}
:

\displaystyle\bm{M}(\bm{I}-\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{sol}(\bm{v}))^{\rm T}=\

\displaystyle\bm{M},

where

\displaystyle\bm{M}:=\

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}^{\rm T}\left(\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}^{\rm T}\right)^{-1}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}.

Then, one carries out a series of "simple" manipulations:

\displaystyle\bm{M}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{sol}(\bm{v})^{\rm T}=\

\displaystyle\bm{0},

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{sol}(\bm{v})\bm{M}=\

\displaystyle\bm{0},

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{sol}(\bm{v})\bm{M}\delta\bm{v}=\

\displaystyle\bm{0},

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{sol}(\bm{v})(\bm{M}\delta\bm{v}-\delta\bm{v}+\bm{I}\delta\bm{v})=\

\displaystyle\bm{0},

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{sol}(\bm{v})\delta\bm{v}=\

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{sol}(\bm{v})(\bm{I}-\bm{M})\delta\bm{v}.

(A.24)

Finally, one combines (
A.16
) and (
A.24
):

\displaystyle\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{sol}(\bm{v})|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\delta\bm{v}=\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\left(\bm{I}-\left(\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\mathcal{L}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet},\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\right)^{-1}\nabla_{\bm{vv}}\mathcal{L}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\right)\left(\bm{I}-\bm{M}\right)\delta\bm{v}-...

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\big(\left(\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\mathcal{L}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet},\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\right)^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{v}^{\bullet},\delta\bm{v})}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\lambda_{(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}^{\rm T}\underbrace{\nabla_{\bm{v}}g_{p(i)}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\big)\big(\bm{I}-\bm{M}\big)\delta\bm{v}}_{=0},

where indeed:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}(\bm{I}-\bm{M})=\

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}-\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}^{\rm T}\left(\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}^{\rm T}\right)^{-1}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}},

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}-\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}},

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\bm{0}.

This only leaves:

\displaystyle\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{sol}(\bm{v})|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\delta\bm{v}=\

\displaystyle\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\left(\bm{I}-\left(\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\mathcal{L}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet},\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\right)^{-1}\nabla_{\bm{vv}}\mathcal{L}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\right)\left(\bm{I}-\bm{M}\right)\delta\bm{v},

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{A}\delta\bm{v}-\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{B}\delta\bm{v}.

where

\displaystyle\bm{A}:=\

\displaystyle\bm{I}-\bm{M},

\displaystyle\bm{B}:=\

\displaystyle\left(\left(\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\mathcal{L}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet},\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\right)^{-1}\nabla_{\bm{vv}}\mathcal{L}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\right)\left(\bm{I}-\bm{M}\right).

(A.25)

Let us now analyze the terms
\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{A}\delta\bm{v}
and
\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{B}\delta\bm{v}
. Let’s start with the first one:

\frac{\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{A}\delta\bm{v}}{\|\delta\bm{v}\|^{2}}=1-\frac{\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{M}\delta\bm{v}}{\|\delta\bm{v}\|^{2}},\qquad\qquad\frac{\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{A}\delta\bm{v}}{\|\delta\bm{v}\|^{2}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}=1,&\text{ if $\bm{M}\delta\bm{v}=0$},\\
<1,&\text{ if $\bm{M}\delta\bm{v}\neq 0$}.\\
\end{array}\right.

(A.26)

As the model is systematically convex at the correction point, see (
2.43
):

\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\mathcal{L}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet},\bm{v}^{\bullet}}>0,

(A.27)

\displaystyle\bullet\text{ If $\bm{v}^{\bullet}$ is a minimum of the plant: }

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{vv}}\mathcal{L}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}>0,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle(\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\mathcal{L}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet},\bm{v}^{\bullet}})^{-1}\nabla_{\bm{vv}}\mathcal{L}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}>0,\qquad\

(A.28)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle(\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\mathcal{L}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet},\bm{v}^{\bullet}})^{-1}\nabla_{\bm{vv}}\mathcal{L}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}(\bm{I}-\bm{M})\geq 0,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{B}\delta\bm{v}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}=0,&\text{if $(\bm{I}-\bm{M})\delta\bm{v}=0$},\\
>0,&\text{if $(\bm{I}-\bm{M})\delta\bm{v}\neq 0$}.\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\bullet\text{ If $\bm{v}^{\bullet}$ is a maximum of the plant: }

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{B}\delta\bm{v}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}=0,&\text{if $(\bm{I}-\bm{M})\delta\bm{v}=0$},\\
<0,&\text{if $(\bm{I}-\bm{M})\delta\bm{v}\neq 0$}.\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\bullet\text{ If $\bm{v}^{\bullet}$ is a saddle-point of the plant: }

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{B}\delta\bm{v}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}=0,&\text{if $(\bm{I}-\bm{M})\delta\bm{v}=0$},\\
\gtrless 0,&\text{if $(\bm{I}-\bm{M})\delta\bm{v}\neq 0$}.\end{array}\right.

And by combining these results with (
A.26
) one finds that
\forall\delta\bm{v}\in\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{u}}\backslash\bm{0}
:

\displaystyle\bullet\text{ If $\bm{v}^{\bullet}$ is a minimum of the plant: }

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{A}\delta\bm{v}-\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{B}\delta\bm{v}}{\|\delta\bm{v}\|^{2}}<1,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{sol}(\bm{v})|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\delta\bm{v}}{\|\delta\bm{v}\|^{2}}<1.

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\nabla^{S}(\bm{v}^{\bullet},\delta\bm{v})<1.\phantom{\underbrace{a}_{a}}

\displaystyle\bullet\text{ If $\bm{v}^{\bullet}$ is a maximum of the plant:}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\nabla^{S}(\bm{v}^{\bullet},\delta\bm{v})>1,

\displaystyle\bullet\text{ If $\bm{v}^{\bullet}$ is a saddle-point of the plant: }

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\nabla^{S}(\bm{v}^{\bullet},\delta\bm{v})\gtrless 1.

This concludes the proof of statement B.

Finally, one can reuse the results of the previous analysis to prove statement C:

\displaystyle\nabla^{S}(\bm{v},\delta\bm{v})<\

\displaystyle-1,

(A.35)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{A}\delta\bm{v}-\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{B}\delta\bm{v}}{\|\delta\bm{v}\|^{2}}<\

\displaystyle-1,

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\bm{B}\delta\bm{v}}{\|\delta\bm{v}\|^{2}}<\

\displaystyle-1,

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\left((\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\mathcal{L}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet},\bm{v}^{\bullet}})^{-1}\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\mathcal{L}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\right)\left(\bm{I}-\bm{M}\right)\delta\bm{v}}{\|\delta\bm{v}\|^{2}}<\

\displaystyle-1,

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\left((\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\mathcal{L}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet},\bm{v}^{\bullet}})^{-1}\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\mathcal{L}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\right)\delta\bm{v}}{\|\delta\bm{v}\|^{2}}<\

\displaystyle-1,

\displaystyle\frac{\delta\bm{v}^{\rm T}\left((\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\mathcal{L}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet},\bm{v}^{\bullet}})^{-1}\nabla_{\bm{uu}}\mathcal{L}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\right)\delta\bm{v}}{\|\delta\bm{v}\|^{2}}>\

\displaystyle 2.

(A.36)

Where one mainly uses the fact that as
\delta\bm{v}
is a vector orthogonal to the set of active constraints of the plant at
\bm{v}^{\bullet}
:

\bm{M}\delta\bm{v}=\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}^{\rm T}\left(\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}^{\rm T}\right)^{-1}\underbrace{\nabla_{\bm{v}}\bm{g}^{a}_{p}|_{\bm{v}^{\bullet}}\delta\bm{v}}_{=\bm{0}}=\bm{0}.

The equivalence (
A.35
)
\Leftrightarrow
(
A.36
) demonstrates (
2.46
). Which concludes the proof of the Theorem
2.3
.

Appendix B
The Tennessee Eastman challenge process

The TE model is described in Figures
B.1
and
B.2
and the notation is explained in Table
B.1
. The four sub-costs are:

\displaystyle\phi_{(1)}=0.0536\ W_{comp},

\displaystyle\phi_{(2)}=0.0318\ \dot{m}_{steam},

\displaystyle\phi_{(3)}=\frac{\dot{V}_{9}\rho_{9}}{M_{9}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=A\\
i\neq B\end{subarray}}^{H}C_{i}c_{i,9},

\displaystyle\phi_{(4)}=\frac{\dot{V}_{11}\rho_{11}}{M_{11}}\sum_{i=D}^{F}C_{i}c_{i,11},

where
C_{i}
are the costs of the components
i=A,\ldots,H
listed in Table
B.2
. The inequality constraints are:

\displaystyle\bm{g}=\ \Big[

\displaystyle g_{(1)}:=T_{r}-150,\quad g_{(2)}:=P_{r}-2895,\quad g_{(3)}:=V_{r}-21.3,\quad g_{(4)}:=11.8-V_{r},

\displaystyle g_{(5)}:=V_{sep}-9,\quad g_{(6)}:=3.3-V_{sep},\quad g_{(7)}:=V_{str}-6.6,\quad g_{(8)}:=3.5-V_{str},

\displaystyle g_{(9)}:=\frac{c_{G,11}M_{G}}{c_{H,11}M_{H}}-1,\quad g_{(10)}:=1-\frac{c_{G,11}M_{G}}{c_{H,11}M_{H}},

\displaystyle g_{(11)}:=\frac{\dot{V}_{11}\rho_{11}}{M_{11}}(M_{G}c_{G,11}+M_{H}c_{H,11})-14076,

\displaystyle g_{(12)}:=14076-\frac{\dot{V}_{11}\rho_{11}}{M_{11}}(M_{G}c_{G,11}+M_{H}c_{H,11})\Big]^{\rm T}\leq\bm{0},

where
\{g_{(9)},g_{(10)}\}
and
\{g_{(11)},g_{(12)}\}
are quality and production constraints, respectively.

Remark B.1
.

(An alternative to affine corrections)
For the implementation of IMA-A and -B, the way some of the measured variables have been corrected has been slightly changed. Indeed the (expected) affine-in-input correction of
\bm{c}_{5}
,
\bm{c}_{6}
,
\bm{c}_{8}
,
\bm{c_{9}}
, and
\bm{c}_{11}
for IMA-A was implemented instead as follows,
\forall i=A,\ldots,H
, and
j=5,\,6,\,8,\,9,\,11
:

c_{i,j,m}(\bm{u})=\frac{c_{i,j}(\bm{u})+\bm{\varepsilon}_{k}^{c_{i,j}}+\bm{\lambda}_{k}^{c_{i,j}}(\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{k})}{\sum_{\ell=A}^{H}c_{\ell,j}(\bm{u})+\bm{\varepsilon}_{k}^{c_{\ell,j}}+\bm{\lambda}_{k}^{c_{\ell,j}}(\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{k})}.

(B.1)

Doing so allows maintaining
c_{i,j,m}\in[0,1]
and avoids numerical problems due to unrealistic linear corrections of the molar fractions (i.e. outside
[0,\ 1]
), which led simulations to fail. Still, it is easy to verify that doing so preserved the affine correction of
c_{i,j}
at
\bm{u}_{k}
:

\displaystyle c_{i,j,m}\big|_{\bm{u}_{k}}=\

\displaystyle\frac{c_{i,j,p}\big|_{\bm{u}_{k}}}{\sum_{\ell=A}^{H}c_{\ell,j,p}\big|_{\bm{u}_{k}}},

\displaystyle\nabla_{u}c_{i,j,m}\big|_{\bm{u}_{k}}=\

\displaystyle\Big(\nabla_{u}c_{i,j,p}\big|_{\bm{u}_{k}}\sum_{\ell=A}^{H}c_{\ell,j,p}\big|_{\bm{u}_{k}}+c_{i,j,p}\big|_{\bm{u}_{k}}\sum_{\ell=A}^{H}\nabla_{u}c_{\ell,j,p}\big|_{\bm{u}_{k}}\Big)\Big(\sum_{\ell=A}^{H}c_{\ell,j,p}\big|_{\bm{u}_{k}}\Big)^{-2}.

Since
\sum_{\ell=A}^{H}c_{\ell,j,p}\big|_{\bm{u}_{k}}=1
and
\sum_{\ell=A}^{H}\nabla_{u}c_{\ell,j,p}\big|_{\bm{u}_{k}}=0
, these equations can be rewritten:

\displaystyle c_{i,j,m}\big|_{\bm{u}_{k}}=\

\displaystyle\frac{c_{i,j,p}\big|_{\bm{u}_{k}}}{1}=c_{i,j,p},

\displaystyle\nabla_{u}c_{i,j,m}\big|_{\bm{u}_{k}}=\

\displaystyle\frac{\nabla_{u}c_{i,j,p}\big|_{\bm{u}_{k}}+0}{1^{2}}=\nabla_{u}c_{i,j,p}\big|_{\bm{u}_{k}},

which proves that the aim of the input-affine correction is preserved. To the best of the authors knowledge, this is the first time that a nonlinear correction is performed (for outputs and/or cost and constraints) in the context of MA or MAy, which still allows the reconciliation of zeroth and first order behavior of the corrected and plant quantity.

Appendix C
Technical complements to Chapter 6

C.1
Regression of a Gaussian process (SE-ARD-WN) based on points and directional derivatives

Consider the regression problem consisting of identifying the function
f_{p}\sim\mathcal{GP}\big(f,

\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}\mathsf{f}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j}|\sigma_{f}^{2},\bm{\mathsf{L}})\big)
linking inputs
\widehat{\bm{x}}
to measured outputs
\widehat{y}_{p}
subject to measurement uncertainties
\epsilon_{xy}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{xy}^{2})
. Thus, each experiment result
\{\widehat{\bm{x}}_{\{i\}},\widehat{y}_{p\{i\}},\epsilon_{xy\{i\}}\}
are linked by the following relationship:

\widehat{y}_{p\{i\}}=f_{p}(\widehat{\bm{x}}_{\{i\}})+\epsilon_{xy\{i\}},\qquad\epsilon_{xy\{i\}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{xy\{i\}}^{2}).

(C.1)

In this section one:

(§
C.1.1
)
expresses the functions
\nabla_{\bm{x}_{\{i\}}}^{\mathsf{n}}\nabla_{\bm{x}_{\{j\}}}^{\mathsf{m}}\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}\mathsf{f}}(\bm{x}_{\{i\}},\bm{x}_{\{j\}})
,

(§
C.1.2
)
show how these functions can be used in a GP regression context with points
and directional derivatives
,

(§
C.1.3
)
gives the distribution of the derivatives of a GP (SE-ARD) w.r.t. its hyperparameters,

(§
C.1.4
)
give the upper bounds on the derivatives of a GP (SE-ARD) w.r.t. its hyperparameters,

(§
C.1.5
)
shows how the gradient of a regressed function can be predicted with a GP (SE-ARD).

C.1.1
Derivatives of the kernel SE-ARD

The directional derivative of
f_{p}
w.r.t. the direction
\bm{\mathsf{v}}\in\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{x}}
is defined as:

\nabla_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}}f_{p}(\bm{x}):=\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\frac{f_{p}(\bm{x}+\delta\bm{\mathsf{v}})-f_{p}(\bm{x})}{\delta}.

(C.2)

The covariance functions
\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}\mathsf{f}^{1}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j})
,
\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}^{1}\mathsf{f}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i})
, and
\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}^{1}\mathsf{f}^{1}}(\bm{x}_{i},

\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j})
that characterize the covariance of the pairs
(f_{p}(\bm{x}_{i})

,

\nabla_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j}}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{j}))
,
\big(\nabla_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{i}))

,

f_{p}(\bm{x}_{j})\big)
, and

\big(\nabla_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{i}))

,

\nabla_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j}}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{j}))
respectively are given in
[
77
]
1
1

1

In fact
[
77
]
gives the covariance functions between points and “full” derivatives, i.e. not directional ones. It is however quite simple to deduce the covariance functions between points and “directional” derivatives, which is done here.
and are the following:

\displaystyle\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}\mathsf{f}^{1}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j}):=\

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j}}\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}\mathsf{f}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j}),

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j}^{\rm T}\bm{\mathsf{L}}(\bm{x}_{i}-\bm{x}_{j})\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}\mathsf{f}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j}),

(C.3)

\displaystyle\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}^{1}\mathsf{f}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}):=\

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}\mathsf{f}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j}),

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle-\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}^{\rm T}\bm{\mathsf{L}}(\bm{x}_{i}-\bm{x}_{j})\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}\mathsf{f}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j}).

(C.4)

\displaystyle\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}^{1}\mathsf{f}^{1}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j}):=\

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j}}\nabla_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}\widehat{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{f}\mathsf{f}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j}),

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j}^{\rm T}\big(\bm{\mathsf{L}}(\bm{I}_{n_{x}}-(\bm{x}_{i}-\bm{x}_{j})(\bm{x}_{i}-\bm{x}_{j})^{\rm T}\bm{\mathsf{L}})\big)\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}\mathsf{f}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j}).

(C.5)

And in the presence of measurement noise:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{f}\mathsf{f}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j})=\

\displaystyle\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}\mathsf{f}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j})+\delta_{ij}\sigma_{xy,i}^{2},

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{f}\mathsf{f}^{1}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j})=\

\displaystyle\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}\mathsf{f}^{1}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j}),

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{f}^{1}\mathsf{f}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j})=\

\displaystyle\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}^{1}\mathsf{f}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j}),

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{f}^{1}\mathsf{f}^{1}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j})=\

\displaystyle\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}^{1}\mathsf{f}^{1}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j})+\delta_{ij}\sigma_{\mathsf{v},i}^{2},

where
\sigma_{\mathsf{v},i}^{2}
is the variance of the uncertainty on the measurements of
\nabla_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{i})
.

C.1.2
Regression of a GP with points and directional derivatives

Let’s assemble the available data, i.e. points
\{\widehat{\bm{x}}_{i},\widehat{y}_{p,i},\sigma_{xy,i}^{2}\}
and directional derivatives
\{\widehat{\bm{x}}_{j},\widehat{\nabla_{\mathsf{v}_{j}}y}_{p,j},

\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j},\sigma_{\mathsf{v},j}^{2}\}
, in the following way:

\displaystyle\bm{X}:=\

\displaystyle(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2},...,\bm{x}_{n})^{\rm T},

\displaystyle\bm{y}_{p}:=\

\displaystyle(\widehat{y}_{p,1},\widehat{\nabla_{\mathsf{v}_{2}}y}_{p,2},...,\widehat{y}_{p,n})^{\rm T},

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{V}}:=\

\displaystyle(\varnothing,\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{2},...,\varnothing)^{\rm T},

\displaystyle\bm{\sigma}:=\

\displaystyle(\sigma_{xy,1}^{2},\sigma_{\mathsf{v},2}^{2},...,\sigma_{xy,n}^{2})^{\rm T}.

(C.6)

Then the location of the curve
f_{p}
regressed at a point
\bm{x}_{*}\in\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{x}}
is:

\displaystyle\amsmathbb{E}[f_{p}(\bm{x}_{*})|\bm{x}_{*},\bm{X},\bm{y}_{p},\bm{\mathsf{V}},\bm{\sigma}]=\

\displaystyle f(\bm{x}_{*})+\bm{\mathsf{c}}_{*}^{\rm T}\bm{\mathsf{C}}^{-1}(\bm{y}_{p}-\bm{y}),

\displaystyle\amsmathbb{V}[f_{p}(\bm{x}_{*})|\bm{x}_{*},\bm{X},\bm{y}_{p},\bm{\mathsf{V}},\bm{\sigma}]=\

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{**}-\bm{\mathsf{c}}_{*}^{\rm T}\bm{\mathsf{C}}^{-1}\bm{\mathsf{c}}_{*}.

(C.7)

where:

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{C}}:=\

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathsf{c}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{1}|\bm{\mathsf{V}},\bm{\sigma})&\ldots&\mathsf{c}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{n}|\bm{\mathsf{V}},\bm{\sigma})\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\mathsf{c}(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{x}_{1}|\bm{\mathsf{V}},\bm{\sigma})&\ldots&\mathsf{c}(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{x}_{n}|\bm{\mathsf{V}},\bm{\sigma})\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{c}}_{*}:=\

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{c}(\bm{x}_{*},\bm{x}_{1}|\bm{\mathsf{V}},\bm{\sigma})\\
\vdots\\
\mathsf{c}(\bm{x}_{*},\bm{x}_{n}|\bm{\mathsf{V}},\bm{\sigma})\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\bm{y}:=\

\displaystyle(f(\bm{x}_{1}),\nabla_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{2}}f(\bm{x}_{2}),...,f(\bm{x}_{n}))^{\rm T},

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{**}:=\

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}(\bm{x}_{*},\bm{x}_{*}),

(C.14)

with:

\mathsf{c}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j}|\bm{\mathsf{D}},\bm{\sigma}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}\widehat{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{f}\mathsf{f}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j}),&\text{if case 1,}\\
\widehat{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{f}\mathsf{f}^{1}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j}),&\text{if case 2,}\\
\widehat{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{f}^{1}\mathsf{f}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}),&\text{if case 3,}\\
\widehat{\mathsf{k}}_{\mathsf{f}^{1}\mathsf{f}^{1}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j}),&\text{if case 4.}\end{array}\right.

(C.15)

where each case corresponds to:

Case 1:
The
i
-th and
j
-th data are points;

Case 2:
The
i
-th datum is a point and the
j
-th datum is a directional derivative;

Case 2:
The
i
-th datum is a directional derivative and the
j
-th datum is a point;

Case 4:
The
i
-th and
j
-th data are directional derivatives

In other words:

f_{P}(\bm{x}_{*})\sim\mathcal{N}(f(\bm{x}_{*}+\bm{\mathsf{c}}_{*}^{\rm T}\bm{\mathsf{C}}^{-1}(\bm{y}_{p}-\bm{y}),\mathsf{c}_{**}-\bm{\mathsf{c}}_{*}^{\rm T}\bm{\mathsf{C}}^{-1}\bm{\mathsf{c}}_{*})).

(C.16)

C.1.3
A priori expectation of the value, gradients, and Hessians of the plant

According to equation (
C.5
), in the absence of data the variance of the directional derivative of
f_{p}
in the direction of
\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}
is:

\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}^{1}\mathsf{f}^{1}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j}=\bm{x}_{i},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j}=\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i})=\sigma_{f}^{2}/\ell_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}^{2},

(C.17)

where

\ell_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}^{-2}:=\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}^{\rm T}\bm{\mathsf{L}}\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}.

By similar reasoning, the covariance of the couple
\big(\nabla^{2}_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{i}))

,

\nabla^{2}_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j}}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{j})\big)
is:

\displaystyle\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}^{2}\mathsf{f}^{2}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j}):=\nabla^{2}_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j}}\nabla^{2}_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}\mathsf{f}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j}).

Therefore, the a priori variance of
\nabla^{2}_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{i})
is:

\displaystyle\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}^{2}\mathsf{f}^{2}}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j}=\bm{x}_{i},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{j}=\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i})=3\sigma_{f}^{2}/\ell_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}^{4}

(C.18)

From (
C.17
) and (
C.18
), one can derive the a priori distributions of the gradients and Hessians of the plant
\forall\bm{x}_{i}\in\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{x}}
.

\begin{array}[]{l@{}l l@{}l}\amsmathbb{E}[\nabla_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{i})]=&\nabla^{1}_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}f(\bm{x}_{i}),&\qquad\amsmathbb{V}[\nabla_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{i})]=&\sigma_{f}^{2}/\ell_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}^{2},\\
\amsmathbb{E}[\nabla^{2}_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{i})]=&\nabla^{2}_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}f(\bm{x}_{i}),&\qquad\amsmathbb{V}[\nabla^{2}_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{i})]=&3\sigma_{f}^{2}/\ell_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}^{4}.\end{array}

(C.19)

C.1.4
A priori upper bounds of the plant gradients

Let’s rewrite (
C.19
) as:

\nabla_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}f_{p}(\bm{x})\sim\mathcal{N}(\nabla_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}f(\bm{x}),\sigma_{f}^{2}/\ell_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}^{2}).

So the absolute value of
\nabla_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}f_{p}(\bm{x})
satisfies the following (triangular) inequality:

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}f_{p}(\bm{x})|=\

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}f(\bm{x})+\epsilon|\leq|\nabla_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}f(\bm{x})|+|\epsilon|,

\displaystyle\epsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{f}^{2}/\ell^{2}_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}})

It is clear that
|\epsilon|
follows a half-normal distribution. According to the properties of such a distribution
[
41
]
, one finds that:

p\left(|\epsilon|\leq\frac{3}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{\sigma_{f}}{\ell_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}}\right)\approx 0.966.

So one can expect that (with a probability of 96.6%):

|\nabla_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}f_{p}(\bm{x})|\leq|\nabla_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}f(\bm{x})|+\frac{3}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{\sigma_{f}}{\ell_{\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{i}}}.

By generalizing this result to all directions of the input space:

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\bm{x}}f_{p}(\bm{x})|\leq\

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\bm{x}}f(\bm{x})|+\frac{3}{\sqrt{2}}\sigma_{f}\bm{\ell}^{-1},

\displaystyle\bm{\ell}^{-1}:=\

\displaystyle[\ell_{1}^{-1},...,\ell_{n_{x}}^{-1}]^{\rm T}.

(C.20)

C.1.5
The expectation and variance of a gradient of a GP regression (SE-ARD-WN)

To calculate the expectation and variance of the value and gradient of the regression of
f_{p}
at
\bm{x}_{*}
, one has to solve the following system:

\displaystyle\amsmathbb{E}\left[\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f_{p}\\
\nabla_{\bm{x}}f_{p}\end{array}\right)\right]=\

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f|_{\bm{x}_{*}}\\
\nabla_{\bm{x}}f|_{\bm{x}_{*}}\end{array}\right)+\bm{\mathsf{C}}_{*}^{\rm T}\bm{\mathsf{C}}^{-1}(\bm{y}_{p}-\bm{y}),

\displaystyle\amsmathbb{V}\left[\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f_{p}\\
\nabla_{\bm{x}}f_{p}\end{array}\right)\right]=\

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{C}}_{**}-\bm{\mathsf{C}}_{*}^{\rm T}\bm{\mathsf{C}}^{-1}\bm{\mathsf{C}}_{*},

where
\bm{\mathsf{C}}
is defined by (
C.14
) and:

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{C}}_{**}:=\

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathsf{c}_{**}^{\prime}&\bm{\mathsf{c}}_{**}^{\prime\rm T}\\
\bm{\mathsf{c}}_{**}^{\prime}&\bm{\mathsf{C}}_{**}^{\prime}\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{**}^{\prime}:=\

\displaystyle\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}\mathsf{f}}(\bm{x}_{*},\bm{x}_{*}),

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{c}}_{**}^{\prime\rm T}:=\

\displaystyle\left[\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}\mathsf{f}^{1}}(\bm{x}_{*},\bm{x}_{*},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{1}),\ldots,\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}\mathsf{f}^{1}}(\bm{x}_{*},\bm{x}_{*},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{n_{u}})\right],

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{C}}_{**}^{\prime}:=\

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}^{1}\mathsf{f}^{1}}(\bm{x}_{*},\bm{x}_{*},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{1},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{1})&\ldots&\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}^{1}\mathsf{f}^{1}}(\bm{x}_{*},\bm{x}_{*},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{1},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{n_{u}})\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}^{1}\mathsf{f}^{1}}(\bm{x}_{*},\bm{x}_{*},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{n_{u}},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{1})&\ldots&\mathsf{k}_{\mathsf{f}^{1}\mathsf{f}^{1}}(\bm{x}_{*},\bm{x}_{*},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{n_{u}},\bm{\mathsf{v}}_{n_{u}})\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle\bm{\mathsf{C}}_{*}:=\

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathsf{c}(\bm{x}_{*},\bm{x}_{1}|\bm{\mathsf{V}},\bm{\sigma})&\ldots&\mathsf{c}(\bm{x}_{*},\bm{x}_{1}|\bm{\mathsf{V}},\bm{\sigma})\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\mathsf{c}(\bm{x}_{*},\bm{x}_{n}|\bm{\mathsf{V}},\bm{\sigma})&\ldots&\mathsf{c}(\bm{x}_{*},\bm{x}_{n}|\bm{\mathsf{V}},\bm{\sigma})\end{array}\right].

Finally, let us name some values of interest of the covariance matrix (
C.1.5
):

\bm{\mathsf{C}}_{**}-\bm{\mathsf{C}}_{*}^{\rm T}\bm{\mathsf{C}}^{-1}\bm{\mathsf{C}}_{*}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\sigma_{*}^{2}&\bm{\varsigma}_{*}^{\rm T}\\
\bm{\varsigma}_{*}&\bm{\Sigma_{*}}\end{array}\right],

(C.34)

where
\sigma_{*}^{2}\in\mathbb{R}
is the variance of
f_{p}
at
\bm{x}_{*}
,
\bm{\Sigma}_{*}\in\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{x}\times n_{x}}
is the covariance matrix of
\nabla_{\bm{x}}f_{p}
at
\bm{x}_{*}
, and
\bm{\varsigma}_{*}\in\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{x}}
is the covariance vector between the value and gradient of
f_{p}
at
\bm{x}_{*}
.

C.2
Domain on which a PG (SE-ARD) is quasi-affine

To determine the size of a domain over which a GP behaves as an affine function, let us take two points and see how much one can separate them until the affine approximation made at one point is not sufficient to predict the location of the other one. Consider the following two points:

(\bm{x}_{1},y_{p,1})
and
(\bm{x}_{2},y_{p,2})
, and let’s define:

\displaystyle\Delta\bm{x}:=

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{2}-\bm{x}_{1},

\displaystyle\Delta\bm{y}:=

\displaystyle y_{p,2}-y_{p,1}.

Then let’s express the value of
y_{p,2}
with a Taylor series of
f_{p}
centered on
\bm{x}_{1}
:

\displaystyle y_{p,2}=\

\displaystyle f_{p}(\bm{x}_{1})+\|\Delta\bm{x}\|\nabla_{\Delta\bm{x}}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{1})+\|\Delta\bm{x}\|^{2}\frac{\nabla_{\Delta\bm{x}}^{2}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{1})}{2}+\mathcal{O}(\|\Delta\bm{x}\|^{3}),

(C.35)

where
\nabla^{\mathsf{n}}_{\Delta x}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{1})\in\amsmathbb{R}
is the
\mathsf{n}
-th directional derivative of
f_{p}
at
\bm{x}_{1}
w.r.t. the direction
\Delta x
.

If one assumes that
f_{p}
has an affine behavior on the segment
[\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2}]
, the the expected value
\widetilde{y}_{\{2\}}
of
y_{p,2}
would be:

\displaystyle\widetilde{y}_{p,2}=\

\displaystyle f_{p}(\bm{x}_{1})+\|\Delta\bm{x}\|\nabla_{\Delta\bm{x}}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{1}).

(C.36)

Now, what one would like to know is how well this assumption works w.r.t.
\|\Delta\bm{x}\|
. To do so, let’s combine (
C.35
) with (
C.36
) and neglect the term
\mathcal{O}(\|\Delta\bm{x}\|^{3})
. A series of elementary calculations gives:

\displaystyle\frac{|\widetilde{y}_{p,2}-y_{p,2}|}{|y_{p,2}|}=\

\displaystyle\frac{\|\Delta\bm{x}\|^{2}}{2}\frac{|\nabla_{\Delta\bm{x}}^{2}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{1})|}{|f_{p}(\bm{x}_{1}+\Delta\bm{x})|}.

(C.37)

One notices that this relative error depends on the choice of
\bm{x}_{1}
. However, one is looking for a relative “global” error that would work regardless of
\bm{x}_{1}
. To get what one is looking for let’s evaluate separately the distributions of (i)
|\nabla_{\Delta\bm{x}}^{2}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{1})|
and (ii)
|f_{p}(\bm{x}_{1}+\Delta\bm{x})|
when
\bm{x}_{1}
takes all possible values that a
\bm{x}
can take.

(i)

According to (
C.19
):

\nabla_{\Delta\bm{x}}^{2}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{1})\sim\mathcal{N}(\nabla_{\Delta\bm{x}}^{2}f(\bm{x}_{1}),3\sigma_{f}^{2}/\ell_{\Delta\bm{x}}^{4}).

(C.38)

In addition,
f
can be approximated by a GP (SE-ARD) whose hyperparameters
\sigma_{f}^{\prime 2}
and
\bm{\mathsf{L}}
. The matrix
\bm{\mathsf{L}}
is, according to Hypothesis
6.2
, common to the GPs of
f
and
f_{p}
. So:

\nabla_{\Delta\bm{x}}^{2}f(\bm{x})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,3\sigma_{f}^{\prime 2}/\ell_{\Delta\bm{x}}^{4}),

(C.39)

where the parameter
\sigma_{f}^{\prime 2}
is the variance of the function
f
, i.e. it is a quantification of the domain of the output space it occupies (note that if this variable plays a role in these “intermediate” calculations, it disappears from the final result). Combining (
C.38
) and (
C.39
) obtains an estimate of the Hessian distribution of the plant at a point
\bm{x}\in\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{x}}
:

\nabla_{\Delta\bm{x}}^{2}f_{p}(\bm{x})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,3(\sigma_{f}^{\prime 2}+\sigma_{f}^{2})/\ell_{\Delta\bm{x}}^{4}).

(C.40)

From this result, one concludes that
|\nabla_{\Delta\bm{x}}^{2}f_{p}(\bm{x})|
follows a half-normal distribution whose known properties (
[
41
]
) can be used to prove that:

\displaystyle p\left(|\nabla_{\Delta\bm{x}}^{2}f_{p}(\bm{x})|\leq a\right)=\text{erf}\left(a\left(2\cdot 3(\sigma_{f}^{\prime 2}+\sigma_{f}^{2})/\ell_{\Delta\bm{x}}^{4}\right)^{-1/2}\right).

Since
\text{erf}(1.5)\approx 0.966
:

p\left(|\nabla_{\Delta\bm{x}}^{2}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{1})|\leq 1.5\sqrt{2\cdot 3(\sigma_{f}^{\prime 2}+\sigma_{f}^{2})/\ell_{\Delta\bm{x}}^{4}}\right)\approx 0.966.

(C.41)

So
1.5\sqrt{2\cdot 3(\sigma_{f}^{\prime 2}+\sigma_{f}^{2})/\ell_{\Delta\bm{x}}^{4}}
is a probable upper bound (at 96.6%) of
|\nabla_{\Delta\bm{x}}^{2}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{1})|
.

(ii)

With a similar approach:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{1})\sim\mathcal{N}(f(\bm{x}_{1}),\sigma_{f}^{2})\\
f(\bm{x})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{f}^{\prime 2})\end{array}\right.,

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\

\displaystyle f_{p}(\bm{x})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{f}^{2}+\sigma_{f}^{\prime 2}).

So,
|f_{p}(\bm{x})|
follows a half-normal distribution and according to
[
41
]
:

\amsmathbb{E}\left[\left|f_{p}(\bm{x})\right|\right]=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}(\sigma_{f}^{2}+\sigma_{f}^{\prime 2}).

(C.42)

To estimate (
C.37
) it is suggested to use (
C.41
) and (
C.42
) as approximations of
|\nabla_{\Delta\bm{x}}^{2}f_{p}(\bm{x}_{1})|
and
|f_{p}(\bm{x}_{1}+\Delta\bm{x})|
respectively. Then one finds that:

\displaystyle\frac{|\widetilde{y}_{-}y|}{|y|}\leq\

\displaystyle\frac{3\sqrt{3\pi}}{4}\frac{\|\Delta\bm{x}\|^{2}}{\ell_{\Delta\bm{x}}^{2}},

(C.43)

is an estimate of the upper bound of the relative prediction error at a point
\bm{x}_{2}
located at a distance
\|\Delta\bm{x}\|
of a point
\bm{x}_{1}\in\amsmathbb{R}^{n_{x}}
when approximating
y_{p,2}:=f_{p}(\bm{x}_{2})
with (
C.36
).

C.3
A derivative

Let the following Taylor series approximation be used:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\phi(\bm{u},\bm{y}+\delta\bm{y})=\phi(\bm{u},\bm{y})+\sum_{i=1}^{n_{y}}\partial_{y_{(i)}}\phi(\bm{u},\bm{y})\delta y_{(i)},\\
\delta y_{(i)}=\epsilon_{i}+\bm{\tau}_{i}^{\rm T}(\bm{u}-\bm{u}_{k}),\\
\bm{y}=\bm{f}(\bm{u}).\end{array}\right.

(C.44)

The derivative of
\phi(\bm{u},\bm{y}+\delta\bm{y})
is:

\nabla_{\bm{u}}\phi(\bm{u},\bm{y}+\delta\bm{y})=\nabla_{\bm{u}}\phi(\bm{u},\bm{y})+\sum_{i=1}^{n_{y}}\big(\nabla_{\bm{u}}\partial_{y_{(i)}}\phi(\bm{u},\bm{y})\delta y_{(i)}+\partial_{y_{(i)}}\phi(\bm{u},\bm{y})\nabla_{\bm{u}}\delta y_{(i)}\big).

Let’s analyze each of the terms:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{u}}\phi(\bm{u},\bm{y})=\

\displaystyle\partial_{\bm{u}}\phi(\bm{u},\bm{y})+\sum_{i=1}^{n_{y}}\big(\nabla_{\bm{u}}f_{(i)}\partial_{y_{(i)}}\phi(\bm{u},\bm{y})\big),

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{u}}\partial_{y_{(i)}}\phi(\bm{u},\bm{y})=\

\displaystyle\partial_{\bm{u}}\partial_{y_{(i)}}\phi(\bm{u},\bm{y})+\nabla_{\bm{u}}\bm{f}\partial_{\bm{y}}\partial_{y_{(i)}}\phi(\bm{u},\bm{y}),

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{u}}\delta y_{(i)}=\

\displaystyle\bm{\tau}_{i}.

From this one can find that:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{u}}\phi(\bm{u},\bm{y}+\delta\bm{y})=\

\displaystyle\partial_{\bm{u}}\phi(\bm{u},\bm{y})+\sum_{i=1}^{n_{y}}\big(\nabla_{\bm{u}}f_{(i)}\partial_{y_{(i)}}\phi(\bm{u},\bm{y})\big)+...

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n_{y}}\Big(\big(\partial_{\bm{u}}\partial_{y_{(i)}}\phi(\bm{u},\bm{y})+\nabla_{\bm{u}}\bm{f}^{\rm T}\partial_{\bm{y}}\partial_{y_{(i)}}\phi(\bm{u},\bm{y})\big)\epsilon_{i}+...

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{(i)}}\phi(\bm{u},\bm{y})\bm{\tau}_{i}\Big).

(C.45)
